{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{switch}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(ii)

(\lambda,0_{X})
is an additive bundle morphism, that is, the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(E_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\lambda\circ\pi_{0},\lambda\circ\pi_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

(iii)

(\lambda,\zeta)
is an additive bundle morphism, that is, the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(TE)_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\lambda\circ\pi_{0},\lambda\circ\pi_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

(iv)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\lambda}

expressing the identity
\ell_{E}\circ\lambda=T(\lambda)\circ\lambda
commutes.

(v)

The following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\circ\pi_{0}=q\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\sigma)\circ\langle\ell_{E}\circ\pi_{0},0_{E}\circ\pi_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

is a pullback which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
, called the
universality of the lift
.

As a shorthand, we will often denote differential bundles by their projection
q:E\to X
. In particular, for every
X
, its tangent bundle
p_{X}:TX\to X
is a differential bundle over
X

[
CC18
, Example 2.4.(ii)]
. An immediate simple observation one can make about differential bundles is that:

Lemma 2.1.4
.

Let
q:E\to X
be a differential bundle in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Then
q
is monic if and only if
q
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Because
q:E\to X
is a retract,
q
is monic if and only if it is an isomorphism.
∎

What this lemma above tells us is that while it
is
the case that we can largely think of differential bundles over
X
as modules over
X
, the trick should be not to think of
E
as directly a module but instead more of carrying a locally linear structure over
X
parametrized by a module instead. We will see below for the case of both affine and non-affine schemes that this is exactly what happens.

We now discuss we review morphisms between differential bundles. There are two kinds:

Definition 2.1.5

(
[
CC18
, Definition 2.3]
)
.

Let
q:E\to X
and
r:F\to Y
be differential bundles in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. A
morphism of differential bundles

(g,f):q\to r
is a pair of maps
g:E\to F
and
f:X\to Y
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes. We say that
(g,f):q\to r
is a
linear morphism of differential bundles
if it is a morphism of differential bundles such that the following diagram also commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TF}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}

For differential bundles
q:E\to X
and
q^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to X
over the same object
X
, we say that
f:q\to q^{\prime}
is a
(linear) morphism of differential bundles over
X
if
(f,\id_{X}):q\to q^{\prime}
is a (linear) morphism of differential bundles.

It is worth mentioning that linear morphism of differential bundles automatically preserve the sum and zero as well
[
CC18
, Proposition 2.16]
, or in other words, a linear morphism of differential bundles is also an additive bundle morphism.

If
\Cscr
is a tangent category, then for every object
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we will write
\DBun(X)
for the category of differential bundles
q:E\to X
over
X
with linear differential bundle morphisms
f:q\to q^{\prime}
between them. If we need to explicitly emphasize the linearity of the maps, we write
\DBunLin(X)
instead, and write
\DBun_{\operatorname{non-lin}}(X)
for the category of differential bundles over
X
with not-necessarily-linear differential bundle morphisms between them. The categories
\DBun(X)
will be of frequent interest to us in this paper and occupy an important tool for understanding the structure of tangent categories. While this statement is generally true, it is in particular true for both Theorems
4.2.1
and
6.2.3
below. Of particular importance for us are some of the
homological
properties of
\DBun(X)
. Many of the results we present below are immediate consequences of the definition of differential bundles (the
\mathbf{CMon}
-enrichment, for instance, falls into this grouping); those results which are
not
immediate are due to Luschyn-Wright as declared.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

Let
X
be an object in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. The category
\DBun(X)
of differential bundles over
X
with linear maps between then satisfies the following:

(i)

Finite products in
\DBun(X)
are biproducts (cf.
[
LW18
, Proposition 4.1]
).

(ii)

Every object in
\DBun(X)
admits finite powers (cf.
[
LW18
, Corollary 4.5]
).

(iii)

The trivial bundle
\id_{X}:X\to X
is a zero object in
\DBun(X)
.

(iv)

The category
\DBun(X)
is
\mathbf{CMon}
-enriched.

Proof.

Items
(i)
and
(ii)
above are due to
[
LW18
, Proposition 4.1]
and
[
LW18
, Corollary 4.5]
, respectively. Item
(iii)
follows immediately from
(i)
because the trivial bundle
\id_{X}:X\to X
is a terminal object in
\DBun(X)
. Item
(iv)
follows immediately from the fact each differential bundle is an internal commutative monoid in
\Cscr_{/X}
and that linear differential bundle morphisms are commutative monoid morphisms
[
CC18
, Proposition 2.16]
.
∎

2.2.
Examples of Tangent Categories and Their Differential Bundles

In this section we present our main running examples of tangent categories and their differential bundles that we will consider throughout this paper. Lists of other examples of tangent categories can be found in
[
CC18
, Example 2.2]
and
[
Gar18
, Example 2]
.

Our first example, while trivial, highlights the important fact that tangent structure on a category is not unique.

Example 2.2.1
.

Any category
\Cscr
is trivially a tangent category with tangent structure
\Ibb=(\id,\id,\id,\id,\id)
.

Our next example, which is expected and arguably the canonical example of a tangent category, provides a direct link between tangent category theory and differential geometry.

Example 2.2.2
.

The category of
\SMan
of smooth manifolds is a Rosický tangent category with tangent structure
\Tbb
induced by sending a smooth manifold
M
to its tangent bundle
T(X)
. For any smooth manifold
X
, differential bundles over
X
correspond precisely to smooth vector bundles over
X

[
Mac21
, Theorem 1]
. As such, we have an equivalences of categories between the categories
\DBun(X)\simeq\mathbf{VecBun}(X)_{\operatorname{linear}}
, where
\mathbf{VecBun}(X)_{\operatorname{linear}}
is the category of smooth vector bundles over
X
with linear vector bundle morphisms. If we relax the condition that our bundle maps need to be linear, we also obtain an equivalence
\DBun_{\operatorname{non-lin}}(X)\simeq\mathbf{VecBun}(X)
, where
\mathbf{VecBun}(X)
is the category of smooth vector bundles over
X
with not-necessarily-linear vector bundle morphisms.

This next example relates tangent category theory to commutative algebra.

Example 2.2.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. The category
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
of commutative
R
-algebras is a tangent category induced by sending a commutative
R
-algebra
A
to its algebra of dual numbers,
T(A):=A[\epsilon]\cong A[x]/(x^{2})
. An extension of the arguments of
[
CL23
, Theorem 3.13]
gives that for any commutative
R
-algebra
A
, differential bundles over
A
correspond to modules over
A

[
CL23
, Remark 3.15]
. So we get an equivalence of categories
\DBun(A)\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
, where
{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
is the category of
A
-modules and
A
-linear maps between them. If
R
is a commutative ring, then
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
is a Rosický tangent category. For full details of this example, see
[
CL23
, Section 3]
.

Our two next examples connect tangent category theory and algebraic geometry.

Example 2.2.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig and let
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
denote the opposite category of commutative
R
-algebras. Then
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is a tangent category where the tangent functor is defined by sending a commutative
R
-algebra
A
to the symmetric
A
-algebra of relative Kähler differentials
2
2

2

If
R
is a rig and if
A
is a commutative
R
algebra, an
R
-derivation on
A
with coefficients in an
A
-module
M
is an
R
-linear map
\partial:A\to M
such that for all
a,b\in A
,
\partial(a+b)=\partial(a)+\partial(b)
,
\partial(ab)=\partial(a)b+a\partial(b),
and
\partial(1)=0
. Only in the presence of negatives can we omit the equation
\partial(1)=0
, as the identity
\partial(1)=\partial(1^{2})=\partial(1)+\partial(1)
simply says that
\partial(1)
is additively idempotent otherwise.

T_{A/R}:=\Sym_{A}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\right)
. By
[
CL23
, Theorem 4.17]
for the ring-theoretic case and
[
CL23
, Remark 4.20]
for the rig-theoretic case, we have that
\DBun(A)^{\op}\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
. An important take-away from this equivalence is that for any map
f:E\to F
of differential bundles over
A
,
f
is linear if and only if
f
is isomorphic to a map of the form
\Sym_{A}(g)
for some module map
g:N\to M
. Now when
R
is a commutative ring, famously,
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is of course equivalent to the category of affine
R
-schemes, and
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is a Rosický tangent category. Moreover, for any affine
R
-scheme
\Spec A
, we obtain an opposite equivalence
\mathbf{QCoh}(\Spec A)\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}\simeq\DBun(A)^{\op}
, where
\mathbf{QCoh}(\Spec A)
is the category of quasi-coherent modules over
\Spec A
. It is also interesting to mention that in
[
GD67
, Definition 16.5.12.I]
, Grothendieck calls
T_{A/R}
the “fibré tangente” (French for tangent bundle) of
A
, while in
[
Jub14
, Section 2.6]
, Jubin calls
T_{A/R}
the tangent algebra of
A
. When
R
is a commutative ring,
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is a Rosický tangent category. For full details of this example, see
[
CL23
, Section 4]
.

We now provide a brief discussion of the tangent category
\Sch_{/S}
of
S
-schemes for a base scheme
S
. A robust and extensively detailed description may be found in the forthcoming work
[
Voo25
]
by the second author of this paper. In the meantime, we will content ourselves by recalling a highlight reel of the structures at play and the ways in which they interact. Of particular interest will be quasi-coherent sheaves of algebras and modules
[
GD60a
,
GD60b
]
, relative
\operatorname{Sym}
and underlying module functors for quasi-coherent sheaves
[
GD60a
]
, and the relative spectrum functor
[
GD61
, Section 1.3]
. An explicit exposition of these topics may also be found in
[
Voo25
]
.

Before we discuss the tangent category
\Sch_{/S}
, let us do a bit of setup. Note that we assume some familiarity with quasi-coherent modules on locally ringed spaces
3
3

3

The original introduction may be found in both
[
GD60a
]
and
[
GD60b
]
while more modern introductions may be found in
[
Sta24
]
. For our purposes, it suffices to treat the category
\QCoh(X)
as the full subcategory of
{\Ocal_{X}}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
generated by sheaves of
\Ocal_{X}
-modules which are locally a cokernel of free sheaves.
. Recall that for any scheme
X
, if
\Fscr
is a quasi-coherent sheaf of
\Ocal_{X}
-modules then we can locally apply the
\Sym
functors
\Sym_{\Ocal_{X}(U)}
to the modules
\Fscr(U)
and obtain a quasi-coherent sheaf
\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(\Fscr)
which is a commutative monoid for the tensor
\otimes_{\Ocal_{X}}
on
\QCoh(X)
. The sheaf
\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(\Fscr)
is also and a quasi-coherent sheaf of commutative
\Ocal_{X}
-algebras. Let
\QCoh(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}})
denote the category of quasi-coherent sheaves of commutative
\Ocal_{X}
-algebras. Then the symmetric
\Ocal_{X}
-algebra functor
\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}:\QCoh(X)\to\QCoh(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}})
is left adjoint to the underlying quasi-coherent sheaf functor
\underline{\operatorname{Und}}_{\Ocal_{X}}:\QCoh(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}})\to\QCoh(X)
which sends a quasi-coherent sheaf
\Ascr
of commutative
\Ocal_{X}
-algebras to its underlying quasi-coherent sheaf of modules. By
[
GD61
, Proposition I.3.1, Corollaire I.3.2]
(cf. also
[
Har77
, Exercise II.5.16]
and
[
Sta24
, Lemma 29.11.3]
) there is an opposite equivalence of categories
\mathbf{AffSch}_{/X}
of schemes
q:Y\to X
with affine structure morphism
4
4

4

A morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
is affine whenever for any affine open subscheme
U
of
Y
, the pullback
f^{-1}(U):=U\times_{Y}X
is an affine open subscheme of
X
.
and the category
\QCoh(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}})
:

\mathbf{AffSch}_{/X}\simeq\QCoh\left(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}}\right)^{\op}

One of the two functors give the equivalence is a functor called the
relative spectrum functor

\underline{\Spec}_{X}(-)
and is defined in
[
GD61
, Section 1.3]
. The composite functor
\underline{\Spec}_{X}\circ\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}
is exactly the functor
\Vbb:\QCoh(X)^{\op}\to\mathbf{AffSch}_{/X}
of
[
GD61
, Définition I.7.8]
(the “vector bundle with prescribed fibres” functor) which associates to a quasi-coherent sheaf
\Fscr
of
\Ocal_{X}
-modules the scheme-theoretic vector bundle
\Vbb(\Fscr)
over
X
with fibres
\Fscr
(cf. also
[
GD61
, Proposition I.7.11.i]
).

Example 2.2.5
.

For any base scheme
S
, the category
\Sch_{/S}
is a Rosický tangent category where for a scheme
X\to S
, the tangent scheme
T_{X/S}
is the relative spectrum of the relative symmetric algebra of the sheaf of relative Kähler differentials,
T_{X/S}:=\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}\right)\right)=\mathbb{V}(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}})
. For full details of this example, see in particular
[
Voo25
, Theorem 3.4.5, Definition 5.2.7]
. For any
S
-scheme
X
, there is an equivalence of categories
\DBun(X)^{\op}\simeq\QCoh(X)

[
CL23
, Theorem 4.28]
. As before, the important take-away is that a morphism of differential bundles
f:E\to F
is linear if and only if
f
is isomorphic to
\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(g))
for some map
g:\Fscr\to\Escr
of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
.

Another family of tangent categories we will occasionally study in this paper are Cartesian differential categories. Cartesian differential categories (which we will henceforth abberviate as CDCs) are categories which were defined originally in
[
BCS15
]
to capture the total derivative from multivariable calculus over Euclidean spaces. While we do not present a full definition of a CDC (and instead defer the reader to
[
BCS15
]
), we focus on the following observations.

Example 2.2.6
.

Briefly, a CDC is in particular a category
\Cscr
with finite products
\times
equipped with a
differential combinator

D
which sends maps
f:X\to Y
to maps
D[f]:X\times X\to Y
, called the
derivative
of
f
, which satisfies seven axioms from
[
BCS15
, Definition 2.1.1]
which capture the essential properties of the total derivative, such as the chain rule. Every CDC is a tangent category where
TX=X\times X
and
Tf=\langle f\circ\pi_{0},D[f]\rangle

[
CC14
, Proposition 4.7]
. In a CDC
\Cscr
, every object is a differential bundle over the terminal object
0
, so
\Cscr\simeq\DBun_{\operatorname{non-lin}}(0)
by
[
CC14
, Theorem 4.11]
. Furthermore, a map
f:X\to Y
is a linear differential bundle morphism if and only if
Tf=f\times f
, or equivalently if
D[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
, by
[
CC18
, Section 3.4]
. Some important examples of CDCs include that Lawvere theory of real smooth functions, the Lawvere theory of polynomials with coefficients in a commutative rig
R
, any category with finite biproducts, and the subcategory of
differential objects
of a tangent category (cf.
[
CC14
, Theorem 4.11]
).

2.3.
Tangent Functors and (Co)Limit Reflection

We conclude this section by proving a relatively elementary, yet powerful, result which holds for arbitrary tangent categories. Our goal is to establish that every tangent bundle functor of a tangent category must necessarily be limit and colimit reflecting. This is a useful step in both knowing how tangent functors interact with isomorphisms (namely that if
TX\cong TY
then it was already the case that
X\cong Y
) and in determining which endofunctors have a hope of being a tangent bundle functor. We ultimately will use this to conclude that in reasonable situations, all powers of tangent functors reflect the tangent-categorical analogue of local diffeomorphisms (Definition
8.1.1
below).

The technical result we present below is elementary and likely in the category theory literature. However, we could not find it upon a literature search and so present it here with a full proof for the sake of completeness. While this is technically straightforward, it illustrates the technical power that comes from having a category
\Cscr
with an endofunctor
F:\Cscr\to\Cscr
and a section/retraction pair in the endofunctor category
[\Cscr,\Cscr]
.

Theorem 2.3.1
.

Let
\Cscr
be a category with an endofunctor
F:\Cscr\to\Cscr
and natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Cscr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Cscr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

for which
\alpha\circ\beta=\id_{\Cscr}
. Then
F
reflects limits and colimits.

Proof.

By duality, it suffices to establish the theorem for limits. We must prove that for any diagram
D:I\to\Cscr
and any cone
X
over
D
for which
FX\cong\lim(F\circ D)
is a limit in
\Cscr
, then
X\cong\lim(D)
. To this end assume we have a diagram
D:I\to\Cscr
, a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Di}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Df}

over
D
in
\Cscr
(with structure maps
d_{i}
given as above rendering all diagrams commutative for
f\in D_{1}
), and a limit
FX\cong\lim(F\circ D)
in
\Cscr
. Assume that we have a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Di}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Df}

in
\Cscr
and note that applying
F
to
Z
gives a cone over
F\circ D
. As such, the cone
FZ
factors uniquely through
FX
as in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Di)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Dj)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Df)}

Now define the map
\rho:Z\to X
via the composition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}}

An elementary computation shows that the map
\rho
forces the cone
Z
to factor through
X
and so that
X
is a weak limit of the diagram
D
.

To finally conclude that
X
is a limit of
D
, assume that we have two cone maps making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Di}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Df}

commute for all
i\in I
. But then we compute that on one hand for all
i\in I
,

Fd_{i}\circ\beta_{X}\circ\rho=Fd_{i}\circ F\rho\circ\beta_{Z}=F\varphi_{i}\circ\beta_{Z}

and similarly
Fd_{i}\circ\beta_{X}\circ\sigma=F\varphi_{i}\circ\beta_{Z}.
But this implies that both
\beta_{X}\circ\sigma
and
\beta_{X}\circ\rho
are cone maps which factor as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Di)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Dj)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\varphi_{j}\circ\beta_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}\circ\beta_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fd_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Df)}

and so must coincide by virtue of
FX
being a limit of
F\circ D
. However, this gives that
\beta_{X}\circ\sigma=\beta_{X}\circ\rho
, so since
\beta_{X}
is a section and hence a split monic, it must also be the case that
\rho=\sigma
. Thus
X
is a limit to
D
in
\Cscr
as desired.
∎

Corollary 2.3.2
.

For any tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
the tangent functor
T
and all its powers
T^{m}
for
m\in\N
are limit and colimit reflecting.

Proof.

Simply apply Theorem
2.3.1
to the bundle projection, bundle zero section/retraction pair
p:T\Rightarrow\id_{\Cscr}
and
0:\id_{\Cscr}\Rightarrow T
and then proceed via induction on
m
.
∎

We now turn our attention to reflecting isomorphisms.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Let
\Cscr
be a category with an object
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and let
\underline{X}:\mathbbm{1}\to\Cscr
denote the functor on the terminal category which picks out the object
X
. Then
Y
is a limit or a colimit to
\underline{X}
if and only if
Y\cong X
.

Proof.

The colimit argument is dual to the limit one and hence omitted. Observe that a cone
Z
over
\underline{X}
is precisely given by a morphism
f:Z\to\underline{X}(\ast)=X
in
\Cscr
. But then on one hand if
L\cong X
via some map
\theta:L\to X
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{-1}\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

is a pullback so
L
is a limit of
\underline{X}
. On the other hand, if there is a limit
\ell:L\to\underline{X}(\ast)=X
then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

admits a unique factorization through
L
. Similarly, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\circ\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

implies that
s\circ\ell=\id
as well and so that
\ell
is an isomorphism.
∎

The next corollary is immediate from the lemma (which shows that isomorphisms are limits and colimits).

Corollary 2.3.4
.

If
F:\Cscr\to\Dscr
is a (co)limit reflecting functor then
F
is isomorphism reflecting.

Corollary 2.3.5
.

For a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
and any
m\in\N
, the functors
T^{m}
are isomorphism reflecting.

Proof.

Apply Corollary
2.3.4
to Corollary
2.3.2
.
∎

3.
Horizontal Descent

In differential geometry the horizontal descent of a smooth morphism
f:X\to Y
of smooth manifolds is defined to be the map
\theta_{f}:\coprod_{x\in X}T_{x}X\to\coprod_{x\in X}T_{f(x)}Y
given by
\theta_{f}(x,v):=(x,d(f)|_{x}(v))
, where
d(f)|_{x}(v)
is the derivative of
f
at
x
in direction
v
. The horizontal descent of
f
provides a system of coordinates between the tangent bundles
TX
and
TY
which allows us to study the properties of
f
local to the coordinates in
X
and transfer these local coordinates to a subbundle of
TY
. The importance of this map in a general tangent category, when it exists, is difficult to overstate: not only is
\theta_{f}
the pairing map of the projection
p_{X}:TX\to X
and the tangent map
Tf:TX\to TY
, it also allows us to record and reflect various structural properties of
f
, as we will see throughout the paper below. In this section we will study the maps
f
in tangent categories that have a horizontal descent and begin to get to know how the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
can be used to study said maps.

3.1.
Carrable Morphisms

An important structural and technical question to answer in tangent categories is when given morphisms
f:X\to Y
, for which differential bundles
q:E\to Y
is it true that the pullback of
f
along the projection
q
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists in
\Cscr
? If it does exist, is it preserved by all powers of the tangent functor? In complete generality, the question has a negative answer. However, in many examples of interest (such as in our main Examples
2.2.2
,
2.2.3
,
2.2.4
, and
2.2.5
) if
q:E\to Y
is the tangent bundle
p_{Y}:TY\to Y
, then it
is
the case that the given pullbacks above exist and are preserved by all powers of the tangent functor.

In the literature it is frequent practice to study and work with the condition that the differential bundle map
q:E\to Y
(or more generally any map
g:Z\to Y
in
\Cscr
) admits arbitrary pullbacks which are preserved by all powers of the tangent functor. These maps are called
tangent display morphisms
, see
[
CC18
, Section 4.2]
for the original introduction of display morphisms into tangent category theory,
[
Gal18
, Section 5.9, Paragraph 3]
for a more explicit incarnation of transverse display systems, and both
[
Mac21
, Definition 6]
and
[
CL25
, Definition 2.1]
for the more modern framework of tangent display morphisms
5
5

5

A historical note for the interested: the core concept of being a display class of morphisms in a category is taken from
[
Tay99
, Section 8.3]
in which Taylor introduced display classes of morphisms for use with regards to fibrations. These maps were set in the tangent-categorical framework by Cockett and Cruttwell in
[
CC18
]
by way of what are there called transverse display systems. These were later rephrased as transverse displayed tangent categories in
[
Gal18
]
and then finally to tangent display systems in
[
Mac21
]
. A recent in-depth study of tangent display systems may be found in
[
CL25
]
.
. In this paper we are content to work with a weaker property that is sufficient for our purposes.

Definition 3.1.1

(
[
CC14
, Lemma 2.7]
; cf. also
[
CL24
, Definition 15]
)
.

Let
\Cscr
be a tangent category and let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\Cscr
. If
q:E\to Y
is a differential bundle, we say that
f
is
q
-carrable
if for every
m,n\in\N
the pullback of
n
copies of
q
along
f
exists and each of the functors
T^{m}
preserve these pullbacks. Moreover, if
f
is
q
-carrable for all differential bundles
q
, we say that
f
is
DB
-carrable
and that
\Cscr
is
DB
-carrable
if every morphism is
DB
-carrable.

The references appearing in the definition above explain our declaration that
f:X\to Y
is
q
-carrable. First, the term “carrable” is French for “squarable” and was used by the SGA school to denote that specific maps admitted pullback squares. Second, by
[
CC14
, Lemma 2.7]
, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

makes the object
f^{\ast}E
into a differential bundle over
X
via the first projection
\pr_{0}
. The differential bundle on
f^{\ast}(E)
is given as follows: the addition is
f^{\ast}(\sigma)=\id_{X}\times\sigma
, the zero is
f^{\ast}(z)=\id_{X}\times z
, and the lift is
6
6

6

Subject to the usual issue of potentially pre-or-post-composing with isomorphisms to match up with chosen pullbacks.

f^{\ast}(\lambda)=\id_{X}\times\lambda
. Thus a map
f
being
q
-carrable means not only does the differential bundle projection
q
admits a pullback along
f
, but also that the full differential bundle
q
pulls back (is squarable, in other words) along
f
.

Example 3.1.2
.

For any commutative rig
R
and any scheme
S
, the tangent categories
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R},\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, and
\Sch_{/S}
are all
DB
-carrable.

Example 3.1.3
.

It is tempting to think that having every morphism be
DB
-carrable implies that the tangent category
\Cscr
is finitely complete. However, this is not the case. Indeed let us now explain how
\SMan
is
DB
-carrable, i.e., for any vector bundle
q:E\to M
, arbitrary pullbacks along
q
exist and are preserved by
T
. To go about this we begin with a quick observation: submersions in
\SMan
are tangent display morphisms in the sense that arbitrary pullbacks against submersions exist and are preserved by all powers of the tangent bundle functor. This follows from
[
KMS93
, Lemma 2.19, Theorem 35.13.2]
and the fact that submersions
s:X\to Y
meet arbitrary maps
f:Z\to Y
transversally. As such, it suffices to argue that for any differential bundle
q:E\to M
that the projection
q
is a submersion. However, this is well-known because vector bundles
q:E\to M
admit the zero section
\zeta:M\to E
, the section/retraction pair
T\zeta:TM\to TE
and
Tq:TE\to TM
gives the local surjectivity of
Tq
. Thus every vector bundle projection is a submersion and so arbitrary pullbacks against
q
exist and are preserved by all powers of
T
. In particular, this shows that
\SMan
is
DB
-carrable. However, it is well-known that
\SMan
does not have all pullbacks: for example, the point map
0:\{\ast\}\to\R,\ast\mapsto 0
does not have a pullback against the map
f:\R^{2}\to\R
given by
f(x,y):=xy
.

It is worth observing that a tangent category
\Cscr
is
DB
-carrable if and only if there is a differential bundle pseudofunctor

\DBun(-):\Cscr^{\op}\to\mathfrak{Cat}

where
\mathfrak{Cat}
is the 2-category of categories, such that for all morphisms
f:X\to Y
the transition functor
\DBun(f):\DBun(Y)\to\DBun(X)
is given by the categorical pullback against
f
in
\Cscr
. In general however, while not every morphism in a tangent category is
DB
-carrable, the
DB
-carrable morphisms form a subcategory.

Lemma 3.1.4
.

For a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, the
DB
-carrable morphisms form a subcategory of
\Cscr
.

Proof.

We need explain why identity morphisms are
DB
-carrable, and why
DB
-carrable morphisms are closed under composition. That isomorphisms are
DB
-carrable is trivial to see, as if
f:X\to Y
is an isomorphism and
q:E\to Y
is a differential bundle then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}\circ q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square. So in particular identity morphisms are
DB
-carrable. To see that
DB
-carrable morphisms compose, assume that
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are
DB
-carrable and let
q:E\to Z
be a differential bundle. Now consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(g^{\ast}E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\ast}E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in which each square is a pullback and each vertical morphism is a differential bundle projection by virtue of
f
and
g
being
DB
-carrable. But then by the well-known Pullback Lemma the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(g^{\ast}E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}\circ\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}

is a pullback square, so
g\circ f
is
DB
-carrable.
∎

3.2.
Horizontal Descent

Recall that for every object
X
in a tangent category
\Cscr
, its tangent bundle
p_{X}:TX\to X
is a differential bundle over
X
. As shorthand, if a morphism
f:X\to Y
is
p_{X}
-carrable, we will write that
f
is a
p
-carrable morphism. Now if
f:X\to Y
is a
p
-carrable morphism, the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

admits a factorization:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

A large portion of this paper can be rephrased as studying the tangent-categorical structure of various morphisms
f
in terms of said horizontal descent of
f
, so we formally define said terms now.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
be a tangent category and let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable morphism. We call the pullback
f^{\ast}(TY)\cong X\times_{Y}TY
the
horizontal bundle of
f
. If
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
is the unique morphism rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutative, then we say that
\theta_{f}=\langle p_{X},Tf\rangle
is the
horizontal descent of
f
.

Example 3.2.2
.

In
\SMan
, for a smooth morphism
f:X\to Y
, its horizontal bundle can be understood in local coordinates as pairs
(x,\overrightarrow{w})
of a point
x\in X
and a tangent vector
\overrightarrow{w}
of
f(x)
. Then its horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
, acts on a tangent vector
(x,\overrightarrow{v})\in TX
as
\theta_{f}\left(x,\overrightarrow{v}\right)=\left(x,D[f](x)\cdot\overrightarrow{v}\right)
.

Example 3.2.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In the tangent category
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
, the horizontal bundle of a map
f:A\to B
is the rig which is the “infinitesimal extension of
A
by
B
.” That is,
A\times_{B}B[\epsilon]
is isomorphic to the rig
A\times_{B}B[\epsilon]\cong\{(a,b)\;|\;a\in A,b\in B\}=:A\ltimes B
, where
A\ltimes B
denotes the semidirect product. In particular, the multiplication of
A\ltimes B
is given
(a,b)(x,y)=(ax,f(a)y+bf(x))
, so
A
acts on
B
with the dual numbers multiplication on the
B
-component. Then its horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:A[\epsilon]\to A\ltimes B
is defined as
\theta_{f}\left(a+a^{\prime}\epsilon\right)=(a,f(a^{\prime}))
.

Example 3.2.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In the tangent category
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
given a map
f^{\op}:B\to A
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, which we use as the representation of the map
f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
, the horizontal bundle of
f^{\op}
is represented by the algebra
\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}})\otimes_{A}B\cong T_{A/R}\times_{A}B
; this is seen via the calculation:

f^{\op}:B\to A
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}

f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}

\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}a\otimes b\mapsto b\mathrm{d}(f(a))}\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
in
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}

\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B)\to\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}})
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{B}

\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B)\cong\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}})\otimes_{A}B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{B}

\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}})\otimes_{A}B\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}a\otimes b\mapsto b\mathrm{d}(f(a))}\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}})
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}

\theta_{f}:\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}})\to\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}})\otimes_{A}B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}

\theta_{f}:T_{B/R}\to T_{A/R}\times_{A}B

where
\mathrm{d}-
are the generating elements of the Kähler differentials. In this case we also see that the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
is the (opposite) of the map induced by
\mathrm{d}a\otimes b\mapsto b\mathrm{d}\big(f(a)\big).

Example 3.2.5
.

Let
S
be a scheme. The construction of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
for
\Sch_{/S}
is much the same as the construction of
\theta_{f}
for
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
above, save we depend on some slight differences in structure. Recall that the relative cotangent sequence for schemes
[
Har77
, Proposition II.8.11]
says that for any morphisms
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to S
the sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is exact. In this case the tangent scheme of
X
is
T_{X/S}:=\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}))
and the horizontal bundle for
f:X\to Y
is the scheme

\displaystyle T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X

\displaystyle\cong\underline{\Spec}_{Y}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{Y}}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})\right)\times_{Y}X\cong\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(f^{-1}\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{Y}}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})\otimes_{f^{-1}\Ocal_{Y}}\Ocal_{X}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(f^{-1}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\otimes_{f^{-1}\Ocal_{Y}}\Ocal_{X}\right)\right)=\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\right)\right).

The horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:T_{X/S}\to T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X
is then the map

\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}\right)\right)\xrightarrow{\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(v_{X/Y/S}))}\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\right)\right).

Example 3.2.6
.

Let
\Cscr
be a CDC. Then every map
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable because the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is always a pullback (where recall that
TY=Y\times Y
). In this case the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:X\times X\to X\times Y
is
\theta_{f}\colon=\langle\pi_{0},D[f]\rangle
. In particular, if
f:X\to Y
is linear, then
\theta_{f}=\id_{X}\times f
.

Let us now prove some structural properties of the horizontal descent maps with regards to how they interact with compositions, isomorphisms, and the tangent functor itself. For the remainder of this section we work in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an isomorphism then
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
is an isomorphism. Moreover, in such a situation we can take
\theta_{f}
to be the identity.

Proof.

If
f:X\to Y
is an isomorphism then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback because
Tf
is an isomorphism as well. Thus in this case we have that
TX\cong f^{\ast}(TY)
. The final claim of the lemma follows upon simply making
f^{\ast}(TY):=TX
, which may be done freely.
∎

Lemma 3.2.8
.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be
p
-carrable morphisms such that
g\circ ff
is also
p
-carrable. Then the following equality holds:

\theta_{g\circ f}=\gamma\circ(\id_{X}\times\theta_{g})\circ\theta_{f}

where
\gamma:X\times_{Y}(Y\times_{Z}TZ)\xrightarrow{\cong}X\times_{Z}TZ
is the (unique) natural isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that
\theta_{g\circ f}
is the unique morphism making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg\circ Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}

commute. A routine check shows that the composite

TX\xrightarrow{\theta_{f}}X\times_{Y}TY\xrightarrow{\id_{X}\times\theta_{g}}X\times_{Y}(Y\times_{Z}TZ)\xrightarrow[\cong]{\gamma}X\times_{Z}TZ

does precisely this.
∎

When examining how the tangent functor interacts with the horizontal descent of a
p
-carrable morphism, we are obliged to compare
T\theta_{f}
with
\theta_{f}
. As such, we must confront the fact that there is some subtlety in the way that
T\theta_{f}
and
\theta_{Tf}
interact. On one hand, we have that
T\theta_{f}:T^{2}X\to T(f^{\ast}(TY))\cong T(X\times_{Y}TZ)
, while
\theta_{Tf}:T^{2}X\to T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y).
When
T
preserves the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

this amounts to the difference between the unique morphisms making the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!T(\theta_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{Tf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

In particular, note that the morphisms which make
T^{2}Y
into a differential
TY
-bundle are
different
in each case. As such, while the underlying objects
T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)
and
T(f^{\ast}TY)
are certainly isomorphic, the isomorphism between them which translates between
T(\theta_{f})
and
\theta_{Tf}
must also mediate between the different differential bundle structures on
T^{2}Y
based on bundle projections
(T\ast p)_{Y}
or
(p\ast T)_{Y}
, respectively.

Proposition 3.2.9
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable morphism. Then there is an isomorphism
\tilde{c}:T(f^{\ast}TY)\to T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)
for which the following equality holds:

\theta_{Tf}\circ c_{X}=\tilde{c}\circ T\theta_{f}

Proof.

Recall that the canonical flip
c:T^{2}\Rightarrow T^{2}
is a natural involution (and hence isomorphism) which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{Y}}

This then implies that there is an isomorphism
\tilde{c}:T(f^{\ast}TY)\to T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)
which make the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Tf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\tilde{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f)^{\ast}(T^{2}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{Tf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\tilde{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}

Picking out the common face of both triangular prisms gives the equation claimed in the proposition.
∎

Some immediate but useful consequences of this proposition extended to arbitrary powers of the tangent functor is given below.

Corollary 3.2.10
.

For any
m\in\N
if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable then so too is
T^{m}f:T^{m}X\to T^{m}Y
.

Proof.

Begin by observing that the
m=0
case is a tautology while the
m=1
case follows immediately from Proposition
3.2.9
. Assume
m\geq 2
and note that we induce natural isomorphisms
\tilde{c}:T^{m+1}\Rightarrow T^{m+1}
by pre-and-post-whiskering the canonical flip
c
with the various powers of
T
in order to witness that the cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

is isomorphic to the cospan:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

Since
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{T^{m}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

exists for all
m\in\N
because
T^{m}f
admits a pullback against
(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}
for all
m\in\N
and so, by passing through the cospan isomorphisms,
T^{m}f
also admits a pullback against
(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}
. Furthermore, all powers
T^{k}
preserve said pullback because
T^{m+k}f=T^{k}(T^{m}f)
and all powers of
T
preserve the pullback of
f
along
p_{Y}
. Thus
T^{m}f
is
p
-carrable.
∎

Corollary 3.2.11
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable morphism. Then for all
m\in\N
there exists an isomorphism
\tilde{c}_{m}
for which

\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T\theta_{T^{m}f}=\theta_{T^{m+1}f}\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}.

Proof.

Begin by observing that since
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{T^{m+1}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

exists for all
m\in\N
by Corollary
3.2.10
. Moreover,
p
-carrability also implies that all powers of the tangent functor
T^{k}
preserve said pullback squares for all
k\in\N
. Thus on one hand we have that
T\theta_{T^{m}f}
arises as the unique map rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\big(T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!T\theta_{T^{m}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

commutative. However, a routine check shows that we can induce commuting triangular prisms as in the proof of Proposition
3.2.9
and hence produce a unique isomoprhism
\tilde{c}_{m}:T\big(T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y)\big)\to T^{m+1}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+2}Y)
which renders the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\big(T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+2}Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\theta_{T^{m}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{T^{m+1}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\tilde{c}_{m}}

commutative. The identity encoded by said commuting diagram is precisely the claimed identity
\theta_{T^{m+1}f}\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}=\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T\theta_{T^{m}f}
. ∎

Corollary 3.2.12
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable morphism. Then for all
m\in\N
there are isomorphisms
C
and
\tilde{C}
for which

\tilde{C}_{m}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ C_{m}=\theta_{T^{m}f}.

Proof.

Observe that for all
k,\ell\in\N
, applying Corollary
3.2.11
and using the fact that
c_{T^{k}X}=(c\ast T^{k})_{X}
is an involution gives

\displaystyle T^{\ell}(\theta_{T^{k+1}f})

\displaystyle=T^{\ell}\left(\tilde{c}_{k}\circ T\theta_{T^{k}f}\circ(c\ast T^{k})_{X}\right)=T^{\ell}(\tilde{c}_{k})\circ T^{\ell+1}(\theta_{T^{k}f})\circ T^{\ell}(c\ast T^{k})_{X}

\displaystyle=T^{\ell}(\tilde{c}_{k})\circ T^{\ell+1}(\theta_{T^{k}f})\circ(T^{\ell}\ast c\ast T^{k})_{X}.

Consequently, for any
0\leq k\leq m
,

T^{k}(\theta_{T^{m+1-k}f})=T^{k}\tilde{c}_{m-k}\circ T^{k+1}(\theta_{T^{m-k}f})\circ(T^{k}\ast c\ast T^{m-k})_{X}.

Proceeding recursively in
m
and terminating when
m=0
allows us to deduce that

\displaystyle\theta_{T^{m}f}

\displaystyle=\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T\theta_{T^{m}f}\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}=\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T\tilde{c}_{m-1}\circ T^{2}\theta_{T^{m-1}f}\circ(T\ast c\ast T^{m-1})_{X}\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}=

\displaystyle=\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T\tilde{c}_{m-1}\circ\cdots T^{m}\tilde{c}_{0}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ(T^{m}\ast c)_{X}\circ(T^{m-1}\ast c\ast T)_{X}\circ\cdots\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}.

Setting
\tilde{C}:=\tilde{c}_{m}\circ\cdots\circ T^{m}\tilde{c}_{0}
and
C:=(T^{m}\ast c)_{X}\circ\cdots\circ(c\ast T^{m})_{X}
thus gives that

\theta_{T^{m}f}=\tilde{C}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ C

as desired.
∎

We now study how the horizontal descents interact with pullbacks. In particular, given a
p
-carrable morphism
f:X\to Z
and a map
g:Y\to Z
which admits a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

which is preserved by the tangent functor
T
, if the map
\pr_{1}:X\times_{Z}Y\to Y
is also
p
-carrable then we will study how we can write
\theta_{\pr_{1}}:T(X\times_{Z}Y)\to\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)
in terms of
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TX)
.

Proposition 3.2.13
.

Let
f:X\to Z
be a
p
-carrable morphism and
g:Y\to Z
, for which the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists and is preserved by the tangent functor
T
. If
\pr_{1}
is
p
-carrable, then there is a morphism
\hat{\pi}:\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)\to f^{\ast}(TZ)
which makes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

into a pullback square and the following equality holds:

\theta_{\pr_{1}}=\rho\circ(\theta_{f}\times\id_{TY})\circ\varphi.

where
\rho:f^{\ast}(TZ)\times_{TZ}TY\to\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TZ)
and
\varphi:T(X\times_{Y}Z)\to TX\times_{TY}TZ
are the unique induced isomorphisms.

Proof.

The final statement of the proposition is completely formal, so it suffices to first construct the morphism
\hat{\pi}:\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)\to f^{\ast}(TZ)
and then prove that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

is a pullback. To construct
\hat{\pi}
, we note first that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes, making
(\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY),\pr_{0}\circ\pr_{0},Tg\circ\pr_{1})
into a cone over
X\xrightarrow{f}Z\xleftarrow{p}TZ
. As such, we define
\hat{\pi}:\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)\to f^{\ast}(TZ)
as the induced unique cone map, that is
\hat{\pi}=\langle\pr_{0}\circ\pr_{0},Tg\circ\pr_{1}\rangle
. Now assume that we have a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

Since

f\circ\pr_{0}\circ\beta=p_{Z}\circ\pr_{1}\circ\beta=p_{Z}\circ Tg\circ\alpha=g\circ p_{Y}\circ\alpha

we get a unique morphism
\gamma:W\to X\times_{Z}Y
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. But then since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

commutes, we get a unique morphism
\delta
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

commute. We now show that
\delta
makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

Since we already know that
\alpha=\pr_{1}\circ\delta
, it suffices to prove the identity regarding
\beta
. Then we compute that

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}\circ\delta=\left\langle\pr_{0}\circ\pr_{0},Tg\circ\pr_{1}\right\rangle\circ\delta=\left\langle\pr_{0}\circ\pr_{0}\circ\delta,Tg\circ\pr_{1}\circ\delta\right\rangle=\left\langle\pr_{0}\circ\gamma,Tg\circ\alpha\right\rangle=\langle\pr_{0}\circ\beta,\pr_{1}\circ\beta\rangle=\beta.

Thus the diagram commutes. It is also routine to check that
\delta
is the unique such cone map, as it was constructed stage-by-stage from other unique cone morphisms. Thus the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}

is a pullback and hence completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.
The Relative Cotangent Sequence

One of the most important conceptual and technical tools in both differential geometry and algebraic geometry is the relative cotangent sequence. In algebraic geometry, for a morphism
f:X\to Y
of schemes there is an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
. One important application of this sequence is that it can be used to probe for when
f
is formally unramified (this occurs if and only if
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
). On the other hand, by passing through the opposite equivalence
\DBun(X)\simeq\QCoh(X)^{\op}
, we find that the relative cotangent sequence reflects to give a sequence of differential bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}T_{Y/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}T_{Y/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

where the map to the last bundle
T_{Y/S}\times_{X}Y
follows from the following chain of isomorphisms

\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\right)\right)\cong\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(f^{\ast}\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{Y}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\right)\right)\cong X\times_{Y}\underline{\Spec}_{Y}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{Y}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\right)\right)=f^{\ast}(T_{Y/S}).

In differential geometry, for any map
f:X\to Y
of smooth manifolds, we can always study the sub-bundle
T_{X/Y}
of
TX
defined as
T_{X/Y}:=\left\{\left(x,\overrightarrow{v}\right)\in TX\;:\;D[f](x)\cdot\overrightarrow{v}=\overrightarrow{0}\right\}
. This “vertical bundle” not only gives a relative tangent bundle of
X
over
Y
as an object of
\SMan_{/Y}
, but it also provides an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

in the Abelian category
\mathbf{Vec}(X)\simeq\DBun(X).

In both the algebraic geometry and differential geometry settings, we see that once we are able to produce a “relative tangent bundle”
T_{X/Y}
(this is the bundle
\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}))
in the algebraic-geometric setting) we are able to produce a sequence which shows that this bundle
T_{X/Y}
acts as a kernel to the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
. In this section we prove that this is no accident and show that this hold in a general tangent category. Indeed, we will show that provided a map
f:X\to Y
is both
p
-carrable
and

0
-carrable (Definition
4.1.1
), then not only does the bundle
T_{X/Y}
exist, but it
also
is a kernel to
\theta_{f}
in the sense that
T_{X/Y}
is the limit of the following cospan in
\DBun(X)
:

TX\xrightarrow{\theta_{f}}f^{\ast}(TY)\xleftarrow{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{f^{\ast}TY}}X

We also show that this structure provides the framework for defining morphisms we call
T
-unramified, which are the tangent-categorical analogue of a map
f:X\to Y
whose tangent
Tf
has trivial kernel, i.e., no tangent vectors “ramify” through
Tf
by mapping both to zero. While we are primarily focused on the general theory of such maps in this section, they become especially important in Section
6
when we introduce the tangent-categorical extension of immersions:
T
-immersions. There we will prove that in a Rosický tangent category, a map
f
is
T
-unramified if and only if it is a
T
-immersion (Theorem
6.2.3
).

4.1.
Zero Carrable Morphisms

In this section we examine the morphisms
f:X\to Y
for which there is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

which is preserved by all powers of
T
. These morphisms are intimately related to formally unramified morphisms in algebraic geometry and to the vertical bundle of the tangent bundle in differential geometry. They also allow us to produce a new tangent functor on the slice category
\Cscr_{/X}
which is
not
necessarily isomorphic to tangent functor on
\Cscr
“restricted” to the slice category
\Cscr_{/X}
. We will see later in this section that these mutual overlaps allow us to define a differential bundle analogue of the relative cotangent sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of algebraic geometry in an arbitrary tangent category whenever
f:X\to Y
is also
p
-carrable.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. We say that
f
is
0
-carrable
if the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

exists and is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We call
T_{X/Y}
the
relative tangent bundle of
X
over
Y
.

By
[
CL23
, Proposition 2.4]
and
[
Ros84
, Pages 4 – 5]
, if every map in the slice category
\Cscr_{/Y}
is
0
-carrable, then the relative tangent bundle
T_{X/Y}
give a tangent bundle functor
T_{(-)/Y}:\Cscr_{/Y}\to\Cscr_{/Y}
which makes
\Cscr_{/Y}
into a tangent category. These tangent structures are generally different from those which appear on the slice categories in
[
CC14
, Proposition 2.5]
, as those are given by just restricting the global tangent structure to the slice category. Instead, these give a relative version which aligns with the tangent structures used on
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
and
\Sch_{/S}
in algebraic geometry (as opposed to asserting that the only tangents you work with there are induced by the global Kähler differentials
\Omega^{1}_{{(-)}/{\mathbb{Z}}})
.

Let us now see how the vertical tangent bundles
T_{X/Y}
are incarnated in our four main tangent categories of interest. It is worth observing that we can, in complete generality, only say that the vertical bundles exist whenever our tangent category of interest
\Cscr
admits all pullbacks and that the tangent bundle functor preserves pullback
7
7

7

The minimal assumptsion, of course, are that every map is
0
-carrable.
. As such, when we consider
\SMan
we also give a short argument as to
why
the vertical tangent bundles exist.

Example 4.1.2
.

Let
R
be a commuutative rig. Because
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
is complete and that the tangent bundle functor preserves limits, we find that for every map
f:A\to B
of commutative
R
-algebras, the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\epsilon]\times_{B[\epsilon]}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[\epsilon]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[\epsilon]}

always exists and so every morphism is
0
-carrable. A quick computation allows us to describe the relative tangent bundle explicitly as:

T_{A/B}\cong\{(a+x\epsilon,b)\in A[\epsilon]\times B\;|\;f(a)+f(x)\epsilon=b\}\cong\{(a+x\epsilon,f(a))\;|\;x\in\operatorname{Ker}(f)\}\cong A\ltimes\operatorname{Ker}(f).

That is,
T_{A/B}
is isomorphic to the infinitesimal extension of
A
by the module
\operatorname{Ker}(f)
.

Example 4.1.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, for any map
f^{\op}:B\to A
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
we compute that the relative tangent bundle is given by:

f^{\op}:B\to A
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}

f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}

The
B
-module of relative Kähler differentials
\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}
exists

Relative tangent bundle
T_{B/A}\cong\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}})

In particular, every map in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is
0
-carrable because
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
is complete and the tangent bundle functor preserves limits.

Example 4.1.4
.

Let
S
be a base scheme. Because
\Sch_{/S}
is finitely complete and the tangent bundle functor preserves limits, every map
f:X\to Y
is
0
-carrable. In this case we also compute that from the relative cotangent sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

in
\QCoh(X)
, the relative tangent bundle in
\Sch_{/S}
is
T_{X/Y}\cong\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}))
.

Example 4.1.5
.

In
\SMan
we claim that every map
f:X\to Y
is
0
-carrable despite the fact that
\SMan
is not finitely complete. To see this let

T_{X/Y}:=\left\{\left(x,\overrightarrow{v}\,\right)\;:\;x\in X,\overrightarrow{v}\,\in T_{x}X.\,D[f](x)\cdot\overrightarrow{v}=\overrightarrow{0}\right\}

be the smooth submanifold of
TX
generated stalkwise by the kernel of the linear maps
8
8

8

Because the linear maps
D[f](x):T_{x}X\to T_{f(x)}Y
are all necessarily smooth maps of vector spaces, their kernels are closed linear subspaces of
T_{x}X
and so the product space
\{x\}\times\operatorname{Ker}D[f](x)
is a closed linear subspace of
\{x\}\times T_{x}X
. These closed linear subspaces may be patched locally as in
X
because the transition maps in the atlas defining
X
are smooth and hence assemble to give a (not-necessarily of full rank) smooth submanifold of
TX
.

D[f](x)
. The maps
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
and
\pi_{1}:T_{X/Y}\to Y
are then defined by taking
\pi_{0}
to be the (smooth) submanifold inclusion and
\pi_{1}
to be given by
\pi_{1}(x,\overrightarrow{v}\,):=f(x)
. The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

then commutes precisely because each tangent vector
(x,\overrightarrow{v}\,)
in
T_{X/Y}
has been chosen so that the vector
\overrightarrow{v}
lies in the kernel of
D[f](x)
. To see that it is a pullback assume that
M
is a smooth manifold with maps
g:M\to Y
and
h:M\to TX
for which

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

commutes. Now observe that for all
m\in M
we can write

h(m)=\left(h_{0}(m),\overrightarrow{h_{1}^{h_{0}(m)}(m)}\right)

where
h_{0}=\pr_{0}\circ h
and where
h_{1}^{h_{0}(-)}(-)
is the map which assigns to each
m
the tangent vector component associated to
h(m)
anchored at
h_{0}(m)
. We claim that this factors through
T_{X/Y}
; because
\pi_{0}
is monic (it is injective on the level of underlying sets), it suffices to provide such a factorization because it is consequently always unique if it exists. From the fact that
0_{Y}\circ g=Tf\circ h
we see that for all
m\in M
we have

\left(g(m),\overrightarrow{0}\,\right)=(0_{Y}\circ g)(m)=(Tf\circ h)(m)=\left(f\big(h_{0}(m)\big),D[f]\bigg(f\big(h_{0}(m)\big)\bigg)\overrightarrow{h_{1}^{h_{0}(m)}\big(m\big)}\,\right).

This informs us simultaneously that
g(m)=(f\circ h_{0})(m)
for all
m\in M
and also that for all
m\in M
,

D[f]\bigg(f\big(h_{0}(m)\big)\bigg)\overrightarrow{h_{1}^{h_{0}(m)}\big(m\big)}=\overrightarrow{0}

in
T_{f(h_{0}(m))}Y
. Thus every tangent vector
h_{1}^{h_{0}(m)}(m)
lies in the kernel of
D[f]\big(f(h_{0}(m))\big)
and so
h
factors through the inclusion of
T_{X/Y}
into
TX
. This implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

commutes. Lastly, it is not difficult to see that the tangent bundle functor preserves this pullback, and so this gives us that
f
is
0
-carrable.

As a prelude towards defining unramified morphisms in tangent categories, we now argue that whenever
f:X\to Y
is
0
-carrable, the unique map
X\to T_{X/Y}
is monic. So if
f:X\to Y
is
0
-carrable, let
0_{X/Y}:X\to T_{X/Y}
be the unique map which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!0_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

Lemma 4.1.6
.

For any
0
-carrable map
f:X\to Y
in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, the unique map
0_{X/Y}:X\to T_{X/Y}
is monic.

Proof.

Since
0_{Y}
is monic, so is
\pi_{0}
. Since
0_{X}
is also monic and
0_{X}=0_{Y}\circ 0_{X/Y}
, it is immediate that
0_{X/Y}
is monic as well.
∎

Before moving on, it is worth discussing some of the structure maps that the relative tangent bundle
T_{X/Y}
carries, in particular its differential bundle structure. So for any object
f:X\to Y
, the projection
p_{X/Y}:T_{X/Y}\to X
is defined via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}

which induces a differential bundle

p_{X/Y}=\left(\hbox to45.64pt{\vbox to49.86pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.81981pt\lower-24.93056pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-29.32504pt}{-19.8264pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 12.64 L 0 -11.16}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-1.0}{1.0}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-8.2667pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.35277pt}{-0.80275pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

over
X
in
\Cscr
. The addition
\operatorname{add}_{X/Y}
is relatively straightforward to define by pulling back against two copies of the projection
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
and using the addition on
T_{2}X
. To define the lift
\lambda_{X/Y}
, by assumption of being
0
-carrable, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

is a pullback square. But then we induce the commuting cube

(4.1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\lambda_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}

with
\lambda_{X/Y}
given by the universal property carried by
T(T_{X/Y})
. We also have a flip
c_{X/Y}
on the relative tangent structure, which we obtain by following roughly the same process as defining the lift
\ell_{X/Y}
. Once again we observe that upon applying
T
to the diagram exhibiting the universal property of
T_{X/Y}
and appending the naturality square for
c_{Y}
we obtain the commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y}}

Note that both squares are pullback squares, so the overall diagram is a pullback square and hence
