{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y}\circ T^{2}f}

is a pullback. This implies that in particular, as
c_{Y}\circ T^{2}f\cong T^{2}f
in the sense that they differ by an application of
c
, there is a pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

in
\Cscr
. This incidentally shows that
Tf
is
0
-carrable. Moreover, this also shows that there is a unique map
c_{X/Y}
which renders the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0\ast T)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,c_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}

commutative. Putting these observations together allows us not only to deduce that:

Proposition 4.1.7
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
0
-carrable then
Tf
is
0
-carrable. Furthermore,
T(T_{X/Y})
may be used as a model for the pullback of
T^{2}f
against
(0\ast T)_{Y}
.

Putting this together with the fact that tangent functors reflect limits by
2.3.2
, we obtain the following characterization.

Corollary 4.1.8
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, then a map
f
is
0
-carrable if and only if
Tf
is
0
-carrable.

4.2.
The Relative Cotangent Sequence

We now prove the most important result in this section. The theorem below is somewhat innocuous, as on one hand it simply shows that it is not a coincidence that in each of Examples
4.1.2
,
4.1.3
,
4.1.4
, and
4.1.5

T_{X/Y}
had the flavour of a kernel of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
(in fact, in Example
4.1.5
we literally defined
T_{X/Y}
as the kernel of
\theta_{f}
in
\DBun(X)
). However, on the other hand what is remarkable about the following result is that it gives us a way of phrasing the relative cotangent sequence in tangent-categorical terms, allowing us to study said cotangent sequence in any tangent category.

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
0
-carrable and
p
-carrable morphism in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Then the map
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}}

into an equalizer diagram in
\DBun(X)
.

Proof.

We first check that
\pi_{0}
does indeed equalize
\theta_{f}
and
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}
. To see this note that since
f^{\ast}(TY)
is a pullback, it suffices to show that
\pr_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=\pr_{0}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
and
\pr_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=\pr_{1}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
. To do so, first observe that
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
is a linear morphism of differential bundles, which implies that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}\circ p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(0_{Y})}

commute. The diagram involving only projections implies that
\pr_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
while the fact that the differential bundle
f^{\ast}(TY)
has projection
\pr_{0}
and zero
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})
implies that
\pr_{0}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
. So
\pr_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=\pr_{0}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
. In a similar vein, we also get that
\pr_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=Tf\circ\pi_{0}=0_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}
and

\pr_{1}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=0_{Y}\circ f\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=0_{Y}\circ p_{Y}\circ Tf\circ\pi_{0}=0_{Y}\circ p_{Y}\circ 0_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}=0_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}.

Thus
\pr_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=\pr_{1}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
. Therefore, by the universal property of
f^{\ast}(TY)
, it follows that
\theta_{f}\circ\pi_{0}=f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}
and so
\pi_{0}
equalizes
\theta_{f}
and
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}
.

To see that
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
is the equalizer claimed, let
q:Z\to X
be a differential bundle, and let
k:q\to p_{X}
be a a linear differential bundle morphism which equalizes
\theta_{f}
and
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}
, so
k:Z\to TX
. Using that
k
commutes with bundle projections, we have that

\displaystyle Tf\circ k

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ k=\pi_{1}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}\circ k=\pi_{1}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ q=0_{Y}\circ f\circ q

This gives a unique map
\rho:Z\to T_{X/Y}
which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}

We now need to verify that
\rho
is a linear differential bundle morphism. To see this we first note that the cell factoring
k
shows that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}

commutes. However, the upper edge of the rectangle satisfies
q=p_{X}\circ k=p_{X}\circ\pi_{0}\circ\rho=p_{X/Y}\circ\rho
so
(\rho,\id_{X})
is a bundle map. To see that it is linear it suffices to prove that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\rho}

commutes; for the construction of
\lambda_{X/Y}
, see Diagram
4.1.1
. However, as
T^{2}X\xleftarrow{T\pi_{0}}T(T_{X/Y})\xrightarrow{T\pi_{1}}TY
is the pullback of
T^{2}X\xrightarrow{T^{2}f}T^{2}Y\xleftarrow{(T\ast 0)_{Y}}TY
, it carries the universal property that
T\rho
is the unique map which renders the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tk}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!T\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf\circ Tq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}

commutative. Note also that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf\circ Tq\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tk\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

commutes with the outer edges composing into a way to witness
Z
as a cone over
T^{2}X\xrightarrow{T^{2}f}T^{2}Y\xleftarrow{(T\ast 0)_{Y}}TY
. As such, if we can prove now that both
T\rho\circ\lambda
and
\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho
factor the diagram above through
T(T_{X/Y})
, we will be finished with verifying the linearity of
(\rho,\id_{X})
by way of the universal property of the pullback.

We first show that
T\rho\circ\lambda
factors the above square through
T(T_{X/Y})
. To this end we compute now that

T\pi_{0}\circ T\rho\circ\lambda=T(\pi_{0}\circ\rho)\circ\lambda=Tk\circ\lambda

while

T\pi_{1}\circ T\rho\circ\lambda=T(\pi_{1}\circ\rho)\circ\lambda=T(f\circ q)\circ\lambda=Tf\circ Tq\circ\lambda.

Thus the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\rho\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq\circ Tf\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tk\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

commutes. Next we show that
T^{2}f\circ Tk\circ\lambda=(T\ast 0)_{Y}\circ Tf\circ Tq\circ\lambda
factors through
\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho
. To this end we first compute that

T\pi_{0}\circ\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho=\ell_{X}\circ\pi_{0}\circ\rho=\ell_{X}\circ k=Tk\circ\lambda

by the linearity of
k
. Similarly,

\displaystyle T\pi_{1}\circ\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho

\displaystyle=0_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\rho=0_{Y}\circ f\circ q=q\circ 0_{X}\circ Tf=Tf\circ Tq\circ\lambda;

note that the last identity, namely
q\circ 0_{X}=Tq\circ\lambda
, used that
(\lambda,0_{X})
is a morphism of additive bundles in
\Cscr
. However, this also proves that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq\circ Tf\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tk\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

commutes. However, as
T(T_{X/Y})
is a limiting cone, this in turn implies that
T\rho\circ\lambda=\lambda_{X/Y}\circ\rho
. So we get that
(\rho,\id_{X})
is a linear morphism of differential bundles.

Lastly, since
\pi_{0}:T_{X/Y}\to TX
is monic (since recall it is the pullback against the monic
0_{Y}
), we know that
\rho
is the unique linear differential bundle morphism which renders the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\,\rho}

commutative. As such, we finally conclude that
T_{X/Y}\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}TX
is the equalizer of
\theta_{f}
and
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ p_{X}
in
\DBun(X)
as desired.
∎

Definition 4.2.2
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
0
-carrable and
p
-carrable morphism in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. The
relative cotangent sequence for
f
is the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

As products in
\DBun(X)
are biproducts by Proposition
2.1.6
and
\DBun(X)
is
\mathbf{CMon}
-enriched, this is the differential bundle analogue of an exact sequence. In particular, because the trivial differential bundle over
X
(i.e. the identity
\id_{X}
) is the zero object in
\DBun(X)
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

is exact in the sense that it exhibits
T_{X/Y}
as the kernel of
\theta_{f}
as, by Theorem
4.2.1
,
T_{X/Y}\cong\operatorname{Ker}(\theta_{f})
in
\DBun(X)
. It also recovers the relative cotangent sequence of schemes in the following sense.

Example 4.2.3
.

For a morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
, the relative cotangent sequence for
f
in
\DBun(X)
(in the sense of Definition
4.2.2
) is determined by applying the relative spectrum
\underline{\Spec}_{X}
of the relative symmetric algebra functor
\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}
to the cotangent sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Because the functor
\underline{\Spec}_{X}\circ\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}:\QCoh(X)^{\op}\to\DBun(X)
is an equivalence of categories by
[
CL23
, Theorem 4.28]
we see that the relative cotangent sequence of
[
GD67
]
coincides with the relative cotangent sequence of Definition
4.2.2
.

Example 4.2.4
.

In
\SMan
, for any smooth morphism
f:X\to Y
, the relative cotangent sequence of
f
is the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle p_{X},Tf\rangle}

in
\DBun(X)
. Along stalks
x\in X
the sequence above induces the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ker}\big(D[f](x)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{f(x)}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D[f](x)}

of real vector spaces.

4.3.
Unramified Morphisms

There is a special situation which is worth our focus. We have seen in Lemma
4.1.6
that there is always a monic
0_{X/Y}:X\to T_{X/Y}
which serves as the zero section for the relative tangent bundle of
X
over
Y
. A natural question is then to ask when the map
0_{X/Y}
is an isomorphism. In the case of
\Sch_{/S}
this is equivalent to asking
\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(0)\right)\cong X\cong T_{X/Y}=\underline{\Spec}_{X}\left(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}\left(\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\right)\right)
, which happens if and only if
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
(and similarly for
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
for a commutative rig
R
). In the case of
\SMan,
this is equivalent to asking that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

is a pullback. By making use of Example
4.1.5
this amounts to asking that for all
x\in X
,
\operatorname{Ker}\big(D[f](x)\big)\cong 0.
In turn, this indicates that there is no ramificiation within the map
f
in the sense that nonzero tangent vectors get sent to zero through
Tf
. We make this a formal definition and briefly study these maps below.

Definition 4.3.1
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
is
T
-unramified
if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

is a pullback square which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
.

Immediate from the definition is the following result which characterizes
T
-unramified morphisms as the
0
-carrable maps which have trivial relative tangent bundle.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, any
T
-unramified morphism is
0
-carrable and
T_{X/Y}\cong X
.

Proof.

Because the naturality square of
0
is a pullback, this says that
f
is
0
-carrable and
T_{X/Y}\cong X
.
∎

As mentioned above, we will see in Theorem
6.2.3
that for a Rosciský tangent category, the converse of the above lemma is true. So in a Rosciský tangent category, being
T
-unramified is equivalent to being
0
-carrable. For now, this allows us to mention what are the
T
-unramified morphisms in our main examples.

Example 4.3.3
.

In
\SMan
a
T
-unramified morphism is an immersion. This can be seen from Theorem
6.2.3
below and the fact that
\SMan
is a Rosický tangent category.

Example 4.3.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
a map
f:A\to B
is
T
-unramified if and only if
\operatorname{Ker}(f)=0
. To see this recall from Example
4.1.2
that given a map
f:A\to B
the relative tangent bundle
T_{A/B}\cong A\ltimes\operatorname{Ker}(f)
with natural map
0_{B/A}:A\to A\ltimes\operatorname{Ker}(f)
given by
a\mapsto(a,0)
. This map is an isomorphism if and only if
\operatorname{Ker}(f)=\{a\;|\;f(a)=0\}=0
.

Example 4.3.5
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. Then in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, the
T
-unramified morphisms correspond precisely to the
R
-algebra morphism
f:A\to B
which are formally unramified, that is, those for which
\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}\cong 0

[
GD64
, Définition 19.10.2 & Proposition 20.7.4]
. This follows from the opposite equivalence
\DBun(B)\simeq{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}^{\op}
for all commutative
R
-algebras
A
and the fact that the relative tangent bundle
T_{B/A}
of
f^{\op}:B\to A
is represented by the rig
\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}})
.

Example 4.3.6
.

Let
S
be a scheme. In
\Sch_{/S}
, the
T
-unramified morphisms are precisely the scheme morphisms
f:X\to Y
which are formally unramified, i.e., those for which
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
. This follows from Theorem
6.2.3
below and the fact that
\Sch_{/S}
is a Rosciský tangent category.

Example 4.3.7
.

In a CDC, a map
f:X\to Y
is
T
-unramified if and only if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D[f]}

is a pullback square, where
0
is the terminal object and
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Y}:0\to Y
is the zero map (since recall homsets in a CDC are commutative monoids). This follows from translating between the tangent bundle functor
T
to the differential combinator
D
. In particular, if
f
is linear (that is, if
D[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
) then
f
is
T
-unramified if and only if the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback square, or in other words, if
\operatorname{Ker}(f)\cong 0
.

We conclude this section by showing that
T
-unramified maps compose, are stable under application of
T
, and are also stable under pullback.

Proposition 4.3.8
.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Then:

(i)

If
g:Y\to Z
is
T
-unramified then
f
is
0
-carrable if and only if
g\circ f
is
0
-carrable. In this case,
T_{X/Y}\cong T_{X/Z}
.

(ii)

If
g:Y\to Z
is
T
-unramified then
g\circ f
is
T
-unramified if and only if
f
is
T
-unramified.

Proof.

We first prove
(i)
.
\implies:
Assume that
f
is
0
-carrable and consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}

Since
f
is
0
-carrable and
g
is
T
-unramified, both squares are pullbacks preserves by all
T^{m}
. Applying the Pullback Lemma implies that the outer square is a pullback, and clearly also preserved by all
T^{m}
. Hence that
T_{X/Y}\cong T_{X/Z}
, and thus
g\circ f
is
0
-carrable.

\impliedby:
Assume that
g\circ f
is
0
-carrable and consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg\circ Tf}

Because
T_{X/Z}
is the equalizer of
Tg\circ Tf
and the map

0_{Z}\circ p_{Z}\circ Tg\circ Tf=Tg\circ 0_{Y}\circ p_{Y}\circ Tf=Tg\circ Tf\circ 0_{X}\circ p_{X},

[
Ros84
, Pages 4, 5]
and because
Y\cong T_{Y/Z}
is the equalizer of
Tg
and
0_{Z}\circ p_{Z}\circ Tg=Tg\circ 0_{Y}\circ p_{Y}
also by
[
Ros84
, Pages 4, 5]
, the diagram above admits a factorization:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X/Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ p_{X/Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg\circ Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}

However, as the back face of the triangular prism is a pullback and the rightmost square is a pullback, we may apply the Pullback Lemma to deduce that the leftmost square is a pullback as well, which is moreover also preserved by all
T^{m}
. This shows exactly that
f
is
0
-carrable.

To prove
(ii)
we can either defer to
(i)
as a special case or give a more specialized proof by noting that in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}

with the rightmost square a pullback, the total square is a pullback if and only if the leftmost square is a pullback.
∎

Lemma 4.3.9
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
T
-unramified, then
T^{m}f
is
T
-unramified for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the definition, as being
T
-unramified asks that
T^{m}
preserve the
0
-naturality square pullback for all
m\in\N
. Because
(T^{m}\ast 0)\cong(0\ast T^{m})
for all
m\in\N
, the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 4.3.10
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, assume that
f:X\to Y
is
T
-unramified and that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback which is preserved by
T
. Then
\pi_{1}
is
T
-unramified as well.

Proof.

Because
T
-unramified maps are preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
, for
m=1
we obtain the pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

which fits into a commuting cube:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

The commutativity of the cube implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}

commutes with right-most square a pullback by assumption of
T
preserving the given pullback with apex
W
. The Pullback Lemma thus tells us that the left-most square is a pullback square if and only if the total square is a pullback. However, using the commutativity of the cube we see that the rectangle above is equivalent to the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}

The leftmost square above is a pullback by assumption and the rightmost square is a pullback by virtue of
f
being
T
-unramified. Thus by the Pullback Lemma the total square is a pullback; winding this back shows that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Z}}

is a pullback as well. It is easy to see that this will also be preserved by all
T^{m}
as well. Hence,
\pi_{1}
is
T
-unramified.
∎

5.
Tangent Monics

In this section we study what we call
T
-monic morphisms. These are the monics in a tangent category whose image under all iterations of the tangent bundle functor are again monic. They have been briefly studied in the recent paper
[
CL25
, Definition 3.39]
where they were used there to study display monic
T
-étale maps (which were in turn used as a tangent-categorical analogue of open local diffeomorphisms in a tangent category). We use them here as technical tools for both studying the various morphisms defined in this paper and also as a way to characterize
T
-étale maps and monic
T
-étale maps later in terms of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
(Propositions
8.1.2
and
8.2.3
below).

Definition 5.0.1
.

[
[
CL25
, Definition 3.39]
]
In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, we say that a morphism
f:X\to Y
is
T
-monic
if
T^{m}f:T^{m}X\to T^{m}Y
is monic for all
m\in\N
.

Note that by convention
T^{0}(f)==f
, so being
T
-monic implies that
f
is a monic which is preserved by all powers of
T
. We now present a characterization of the
T
-monics in our main examples.

Example 5.0.2
.

In
\SMan
, the
T
-monic morphisms are precisely the monic immersions, i.e., the embeddings. A clean proof of this may be given via Proposition
5.0.11
below.

Example 5.0.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. Then in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
the
T
-monics are precisely the monomorphisms. This can be seen by observing that the tangent bundle functor
T(A):=A[\epsilon]
preserves limits, so it in particular preserves all monics. By appealing to Corollary
2.3.2
we see that
T^{m}f
for
m\geq 1
is monic if and only if
f
is monic.

Example 5.0.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. Then in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
the
T
-monics are precisely the monomorphisms as well. This can be seen from much the same way as the example prior.

Example 5.0.5
.

In
\Sch_{/S}
the
T
-monic morphisms are precisely the monomorphisms. While we can once again use the same argument as in the previous example, an alternative proof of this may be given as an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.0.11
below.

Example 5.0.6
.

Let
\Cscr
be a CDC. Then
f:X\to Y
is
T
-monic if and only if
D^{n}[f]
is monic for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, that is,
f
is
T
-monic if and only if all of its higher-derivatives are monic. In particular, when
f
is linear, then
f

T
-monic if and only if
f
is monic.

Example 5.0.7
.

Here is an example which shows, concretely, that not all monics in a tangent category are
T
-monics. Consider the CDC
\mathbf{Euc}
. Then the monic
x^{3}:\R\to\R
is
not
a
T
-monic since its derivative
D[x^{3}]:\R\times\R\to\R
is
D[x^{3}](p,v)=\left(3x^{2}|_{x=p}\right)\overrightarrow{v}=3p^{2}\overrightarrow{v}
for all
(p,v)\in\R\times\R
. In particular, if
p=0
then
D[x^{3}](0,v)=D[x^{3}](0,w)
for all
v,w\in\R
and so
D[x^{3}]
is not monic. So
x^{3}
is monic but not
T
-monic.

We now record that in any tangent category, the vertical lift and the canonical flip are
T
-monic.

Proposition 5.0.8
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
,

(i)

The vertical lift
\ell_{X}:TX\to T^{2}X
is
T
-monic for every object
X
.

(ii)

The canonical flip
c_{X}:T^{2}X\to T^{2}X
is a
T
-monic for every object
X
.

Proof.

For the vertical lift, by
[
CC14
, Lemma 2.13]
, the universality of the vertical lift implies that
\ell
is the equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

Additionally, all powers
T^{m}
of the tangent functor
T
preserve said equalizer. Putting this all together implies that
\ell_{X}
is
T
monic. On the other hand, for the canonical flip, this is immediate from
c_{X}
being an isomorphism.
∎

We immediately get that
T
-monic morphisms compose, are stable under application of the tangent functor, and are stable under pullbacks which are preserved by the tangent functor.

Proposition 5.0.9
.

Let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be
T
-monic maps in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
and assume also that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
. Then:

(i)

The composite
g\circ f:X\to Z
is
T
-monic.

(ii)

For all
m\in\N
,
T^{m}f
is
T
-monic.

(iii)

The map
\pr_{1}:X\times_{Y}W\to W
is
T
-monic.

Proof.

Claim
(i)
is immediate from the observations that
T^{m}(g\circ f)=T^{m}(g)\circ T^{m}(f)
for all
m\in\N
and that monics compose. Claim
(ii)
is immediate from the fact that for all
k,m\in\N
if
T^{m}f
is monic then
T^{m+k}f=T^{k}(T^{m}f)
is monic. For Claim
(iii)
, note that upon fixing an
m\in\N
, applying the functor
T^{m}
to the given pullback diagram, and using that
T^{m}
preserves this pullback gives rise to the pullback square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(X\times_{Y}W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}h}

Since
T^{m}f
is monic and monics are stable under pullback, we see that
T^{m}\pr_{1}
is monic.
∎

We now show that for any
0
-carrable morphism the unique map
0_{X/Y}:X\to T_{X/Y}
is
T
-monic.

Lemma 5.0.10
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, for any
0
-carrable morphism
f:X\to Y
,
0_{X/Y}
is
T
-monic.

Proof.

For
m=0
,
T^{m}0_{X/Y}=0_{X/Y}
is monic by Lemma
4.1.6
. For
m=1
observe that because
T
preserves the pullback defining
T_{X/Y}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}f}

is a pullback. Thus
T\pi_{0}
is monic after recognizing that
T\ast 0
is monic because
0
is a section and hence an absolute equalizer. Using the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T_{X/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast 0)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T0_{X/Y}}

allows us to deduce that that
(T\ast 0)_{X}=T\pi_{0}\circ T0_{X/Y}
. Because
T\ast 0
is monic by virtue of being a section, the post-composition of
T0_{X/Y}
with the monic
T\pi_{0}
is itself a monic. For
m\geq 2
a routine induction on
m
using the same arguments as for the case
m=1
gives that
T^{m}0_{X/Y}
is monic.
∎

We now show that a
p
-carrable map is a
T
-monic if it is monic and that the horizontal descent is
T
-monic.

Proposition 5.0.11
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable map in a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Then
f
is
T
-monic if and only if
f
is monic and
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic.

Proof.

For the
\implies
direction, since
f
is
p
-carrable, then for all
m\in\N
we have pullback diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f^{\ast}TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!T^{m}\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

induced by applying
T^{m}
to the pullback diagram defining
f^{\ast}(TY)
. In each case, since
T^{m}f
is monic for all
m\in\N
,
T^{m}\pi_{0}
is monic. Using also that
T^{m+1}f
is monic for all
m\in\N
allows us to deduce that
T^{m}\theta_{f}
is monic for all
m\in\N
as well because
T^{m+1}f=T^{m}f\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}
.

For the
\impliedby
direction, in order to prove that
f
is
T
-monic, we must show that
T^{m}(f)
is monic for all
m\in\N
. We show this by induction on
m
. For the base case
m=0
we note that
T^{0}(f)=\id(f)=f
is monic by assumption. When
m=1
we note that since
Tf=\pr_{1}\circ\theta_{f}
,
Tf
is monic precisely because
\theta_{f}
and
\pr_{1}
are both monic since
\theta_{f}
is by assumption and
\pr_{1}
because it is the pullback of
p_{Y}:TY\to Y
against the monic
f:X\to Y
. We now proceed with the induction on
m
. Let
m\in\N
be given with
m\geq 1
and assume that
T^{m}(f)
is monic. We will show that
T^{m+1}(f)
is monic. Now since we have a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}(Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(T^{m}(Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f)}

Then
\pr_{1}
is monic by the Inductive Hypothesis on
T^{m}(f)
. Additionally, applying Proposition
3.2.9
to
T^{m-1}f
and expanding out the induced algebraic equations shows that there are isomorphisms
\tilde{c}_{m}:T(T^{m-1}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m}Y))\to T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}Y)
and
\hat{c}_{m}:T^{m+1}X\to T^{m+1}X
which make the equation
\tilde{c}_{m}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}=\theta_{T^{m}f}\circ\hat{c}_{m}
hold. But as
\tilde{c}_{m}
and
\hat{c}_{m}
are isomorphisms and
T^{m}\theta_{f}
is monic,
\theta_{T^{m}f}
is monic. This then gives rise to the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}(f)^{\ast}(T^{m+1}(Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta_{T^{m}f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

and hence allows us to deduce that
T^{m+1}(f)
is monic.
∎

Corollary 5.0.12
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable and
T
-monic, then
T^{m}\theta_{f}
and
\theta_{T^{m}f}
are
T
-monic for all
m\in\N
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.0.11
,
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic. By Proposition
5.0.9
.(ii), we get that
T^{m}\theta_{f}
is also
T
-monic. Again by Proposition
5.0.9
.(ii), we get that
T^{m}f
is
T
-monic, hence by Proposition
5.0.11
,
\theta_{T^{m}f}
is
T
-monic.
∎

6.
Tangent-Immersions

In this section we introduce
T
-immersions, which are a class of morphisms in a tangent category which structurally describe what it means to be an immersion in terms of tangent categorical data. While we will generically focus on maps which are
p
-carrable, we will give the definition for general tangent categories before specializing. We will also examine how
T
-immersions relate to
T
-unramified and
0
-carrable morphisms later on in this section.

6.1.
Prepullbacks and Immersions

In order to define
T
-immersions, let us recall first what it means to be a prepullback in a category. So in a category
\Cscr
, a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is a
prepullback in
\Cscr
if for any commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

there is at most one map which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\leq 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

A
T
-immersion will be a map in a tangent category such that the projection naturality square is a prepullback.

Definition 6.1.1
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, a map
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-immersion
if the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a prepullback which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
.

We now show that
T
-immersions are stable under composition and pullbacks which are preserved by the tangent bundle functor. To go about this, however, we need to provide a folkloric structural lemma for the sake of completeness. While we cannot and do not claim originality of the result
9
9

9

We have been unable to find it directly stated in the literature, but the second author learned it from Robin Cockett and according to R. Cockett it is a well-known structural result.
, it is a useful technical tool which we will require.

Lemma 6.1.2

(Folklore)
.

Consider commutative diagrams with squares numbered as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

(1)

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

(3)

Then:

(i)

If Square
(3)
is a prepullback then Square
(1)
is a prepullback as well.

(ii)

If Square
(1)
is a prepullback and Square
(2)
is a prepullback then Square
(3)
is a prepullback.

In particular, if Square
(2)
is a pullback then Square
(1)
is a prepullback if and only if Square
(3)
is a prepullback.

Proof.

We first prove Statement
(i)
. Assume that Square
(3)
is a prepullback and that we have a commuting diagram of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}

Because then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

commutes, using that Square
(3)
is a prepullback implies that
\alpha=\beta
and so proves that Square
(1)
is a prepullback.

We now prove Statement
(ii)
. Assume that Squares
(1)
and
(2)
are prepullbacks and that we have a commuting diagram of the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

Now
g\circ k=k^{\prime}\circ f
and
f\circ\alpha=t=f\circ\beta
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

Then using that Square
(2)
is a prepullback, we get that
k\circ\alpha=k\circ\beta
. This then implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}

commutes as well. Using that Square
(1)
is a prepullback gives that
\alpha=\beta
and hence that Square
(3)
is a prepullback as well.
∎

Proposition 6.1.3
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are
T
-immersions then:

(i)

The composite
g\circ f:X\to Z
is a
T
-immersion.

(ii)

The map
T^{m}f
is a
T
-immersion for all
m\in\N
.

(iii)

If the following pullback exists

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
, then
\pi_{1}
is a
T
-immersion as well.

Proof.

Statement
(i)
follows from an immediate application of Lemma
6.1.2
.(i). Statement
(ii)
holds because by assumption all powers of the tangent functor preserve the given prepullback. For statement
(iii)
, consider the commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Because the rightmost square is a pullback by assumption, by Lemma
6.1.2
it suffices to prove the whole rectangle is a prepullback in order to conclude that the leftmost square is a prepullback. To this end observe also that the commuting rectangle above is equal to the commuting rectangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

we see that is suffices to prove that this last rectangle is a prepullback. To this end assume that we have a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Because
TW
is a pulback with projections
T\pi_{0}
and
T\pi_{1}
, to prove that
\alpha=\beta
it suffices to prove that
T\pi_{1}\circ\alpha=T\pi_{1}\circ\beta
and
T\pi_{0}\circ\alpha=T\pi_{0}\circ\beta
. The first of these identities can be read off the diagram directly as

T\pi_{1}\circ\alpha=t=T\pi_{1}\circ\beta.

For the other identity, consider that the computations

\displaystyle Tf\circ T\pi_{0}\circ\alpha=Tg\circ T\pi_{1}\circ\alpha=Tg\circ t=Tg\circ T\pi_{1}\circ\beta=Tf\circ T\pi_{0}\circ\beta

and

\displaystyle p_{X}\circ T\pi_{0}\circ\alpha=s=p_{X}\circ T\pi_{0}\circ\beta

imply that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes. Using that
f
is a
T
-immersion gives that
T\pi_{0}\circ\alpha=T\pi_{0}\circ\beta
and so allows us to deduce that
\alpha=\beta
. So that the naturality square for
\pi_{1}
is a prepullback. Running this same argument for arbitrary
m\in\N
gives that
\pi_{1}
is a
T
-immersion.
∎

When given a
p
-carrable morphism it is significantly simpler to check if is a
T
-immersion since we now show this is the case precisely when its horizontal descent morphism is
T
-monic.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable, then
f
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
is
T
-monic.

Proof.

\implies:
Assume that
f
is a
T
-immersion and that there are maps
g
and
h
rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

commutative. Then
p_{X}\circ g=\pi_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ g=\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ g=Tf\circ g
,
p_{X}\circ h=Tf\circ h
, and both
g
and
h
make the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. But since the naturality square for
p
is a prepullback we have that
g=h
and so that
\theta_{f}
is monic. Finally, using that all powers
T^{m}
preserve the given prepullback and running the same argument for
T^{m}\theta_{f}
shows that
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic.

\impliedby:
If
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic then it is immediate that for all
m\in\N
the naturality square for
(T^{m}\ast p)
at
T^{m}f
has at most one map by virtue of
T^{m}\theta_{f}
being monic.
∎

We now present the examples of
T
-immersions in our main examples.

Example 6.1.5
.

In
\SMan
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-immersion if and only if it is an immersion of smooth manifolds. Recall from Example
3.2.2
that
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
is given by
\theta_{f}\left(x,\overrightarrow{v}\right)=\left(x,D[f](x)\cdot\overrightarrow{v}\right).
Moreover, since
p_{Y}:TY\to Y
is a submersion, we have that
f^{\ast}(TY)\cong X\times_{Y}TY\cong\coprod_{x\in X}T_{f(x)}Y
. Thus we see that
\theta_{f}
is monic if and only if for all
x\in X
, the map
T_{x}f:T_{x}X\to T_{f(x)}Y
is injective. However, this is the case if and only if
f
is an immersion.

Example 6.1.6
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
the
T
-immersions are precisely the injective morphisms of
R
-algebras. To see this note the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(B[\epsilon])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[\epsilon]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

has
f^{\ast}(B[\epsilon])\cong A\ltimes B
. Under the isomorphism
A\ltimes B\cong\left\{(a,b\epsilon)\;|\;a\in A,b\in B\right\}
, we see that
\theta_{f}:A[\epsilon]\to f^{\ast}(B[\epsilon])
takes the form
\theta_{f}(a+x\epsilon)=(a,f(x)).
Thus we see immediately that
\theta_{f}
is monic if and only if
f
is monic.

Example 6.1.7
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
the
T
-immersions are precisely the formally unramified morphisms of
R
-algebras, which recall are
R
-algebra morphisms
f:A\to B
such that
\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}\cong 0

[
GD64
, Définition 19.10.2 & Proposition 20.7.4]
. To see this, consider that the map
\Spec f:\Spec B\to\Spec A
induces corresponding structure map
\theta_{f}:T_{B/R}\to f^{\ast}(T_{A/R})
given by
\theta_{f}=\Spec(\Sym_{B}(v))
for
v
the map in the relative cotangent exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

Now, since
\DBun(B)^{\op}\simeq{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
by
[
CL23
]
, we see that
\Spec(\Sym_{B}(v))
is monic if and only if
v
is an epimorphism of
B
-modules. However, as the relative cotangent sequence is exact,
v
is an epimorphism if and only if
0\cong\operatorname{Coker}(v)\cong\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}
.

Example 6.1.8
.

The above example extends to the full category of schemes
\Sch_{S}
for arbitrary base schemes
S
. Indeed, in
\Sch_{/S}
, the morphisms for which
\theta_{f}
is monic are precisely the formally unramified morphisms of
S
-schemes. Recall from
[
GD67
, Proposition 17.2.1]
that a morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
is formally unramified if and only if the sheaf of relative Kähler differentials satisfies
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
. Recall also from the relative cotangent sequence for Kähler differentials of schemes that there is an exact sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X

\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\otimes_{\Ocal_{Y}}\Ocal_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\to 0

so
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}
is the cokernel of the map
\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\otimes_{\Ocal_{Y}}\Ocal_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}
in
\QCoh(X)
. We now use the equivalence
\QCoh(X)\simeq\DBun(X)^{\op}
of
[
CL23
]
to observe that the equivalences

\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
in
\QCoh(X)

T_{X/Y}\cong 0
in
\DBun(X)

and

Exact Sequence
\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}\otimes_{\Ocal_{Y}}\Ocal_{X}\to\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\to 0
in
\QCoh(X)

Exact Sequence
X\to T_{X/Y}\to T_{X/S}\to T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X
in
\DBun(X)

combine to allow us to deduce

Exact Sequence
X\to T_{X/Y}\to T_{X/S}\to T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X
in
\DBun(X)

Exact Sequence
X=X\to T_{X/S}\to T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X
in
\DBun(X)

\theta_{f}:T_{X/S}\to T_{Y/S}\times_{Y}X
a kernel in
\DBun(X)

\theta_{f}
monic in
\DBun(X)
, as
\DBun(X)\simeq\QCoh(X)^{\op}
is Abelian

Example 6.1.9
.

In a CDC, as
\theta_{f}=\langle\pi_{0},D[f]\rangle
it follows that
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic if and only if
D[f]
is
T
-monic. Therefore, by Lemma
6.1.4
,
f
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
D^{n}[f]
is monic for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, if
f
is linear then
f
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
f
is monic.

6.2.
Immersions and Unramification

We now show that
T
-immersions are
T
-unramified morphisms in any tangent category, meaning that being a
T
-immersion forces a complete lack of ramification in the map
f
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-immersion then
f
is also
T
-unramified.

Proof.

Assume that we have a cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

and note that if we can show that
(C,\alpha,\beta)
factors through
(X,0_{X},f)
then because
0_{X}
is a section (and hence monic), it will follow immediately that
(X,0_{X},f)
is the terminal cone over
TX\xrightarrow{Tf}TY\xleftarrow{0_{Y}}Y
. To this end we compute that

f\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha=p_{Y}\circ Tf\circ\alpha=p_{Y}\circ 0_{Y}\circ\beta=\beta

so the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes. But now consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

To see it commutes, we compute that:

\displaystyle T(f)\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=0_{Y}\circ\beta,

\displaystyle p_{X}\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=p_{X}\circ 0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha,

\displaystyle T(f)\circ 0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=0_{Y}\circ f\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha=0_{Y}\circ p_{Y}\circ 0_{Y}\circ\beta=0_{Y}\circ\beta.

As such
(C,p_{X}\circ\alpha,0_{Y}\circ\beta)
is a cone over
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xleftarrow{p_{Y}}TY
with cone maps
\alpha,0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha:C\to TX
. Because the naturality square for
p
at
f
is a prepullback,
\alpha=0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ\alpha
and so the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

commutes. Finally that all powers
T^{m}
preserve these pullbacks follows mutatis mutandis by recognizing that since
T^{m}
preserves the prepullback giving the
T
-immersive structure on
f
, we can argue as above after applying
T^{m}
to the zero naturality square.
∎

We can use Theorem
4.2.1
above to show that when each
\DBun(X)
is enriched in the category
\mathbf{Ab}
of Abelian groups, then
\theta_{f}
is monic if and only if
T_{X/Y}\cong 0
. This gives an alternative way to characterize
T
-immersions in
p
-carrable Rosický tangent categories as exactly the
T
-unramified maps. To prove this we recall the following well-known characterization of maps with trivial kernels coinciding with monics in
\mathbf{Ab}
-enriched categories with zero objects. We present a sketch of the lemma for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

Let
\Ascr
be a
\mathbf{Ab}
-enriched category with a zero object. The following are equivalent for a morphism
f:X\to Y
:

(i)

The map
f
is monic.

(ii)

For any map
g:Z\to X
,
f\circ g=0
if and only if
g=0
.

(iii)

The equalizer
\operatorname{Eq}(f,0_{XY}:X\to Y)
satisfies
\operatorname{Eq}(f,0_{XY})\cong 0
.

Proof.

For
(i)\implies(ii)
: If
f
is monic then the equation
f\circ 0=0=f\circ g
implies that
g=0
. For
(ii)\implies(iii)
: if there is a map making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{XY}}

commute if and only if
g
is the zero map, then such a
g
uniquely factors through the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathexclamationmark_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathinvertedexclamationmark$}}}_{X}}

Thus
\operatorname{Eq}(f,0_{XY})\cong 0
. Lastly for
(iii)\implies(i)
: assume for some
g,h:Z\to X
that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and note that we have that
f\circ g=f\circ h
. Then
f\circ(g-h)=f\circ g-f\circ h=0
and so
g-h
must factor through
\operatorname{Eq}(f,0_{XY})
. However, as
\operatorname{Eq}(f,0)\cong 0
this implies that
g-h=0_{ZX}
and so that
g=h
.
∎

Theorem 6.2.3
.

In a Rosický tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable, then
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
f
is
T
-unramified.

Proof.

Because
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
is a Rosický tangent category, for every object
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the category
\DBun(X)
has a zero object and is
\mathbf{Ab}
-enriched. From here the theorem follows by combining Theorem
4.2.1
and Lemma
6.2.2
.
∎

While every
T
-immersion is
T
-unramified, the converse is not necessarily true in an arbitrary tangent category. By Theorem
6.2.3
this distinction may be detected only by non-Rosický tangent categories. We conclude this section with an example of a tangent category that has a
T
-unramified map that is not a
T
-immersion.

Example 6.2.4
.

\mathbf{CMon}
is a CDC where every map is linear, so
D[f](x,y)=f(y)
. As such, per the explanation in Example
5.0.6
,
f:M\to N
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
f
is monic, which in
\mathbf{CMon}
means that
f
is injective. On the other hand, by Example
4.3.7
,
f
is
T
-unramified if and only if
\operatorname{Ker}(f)\cong 0
. So to give an example of a
T
-unramified map that is not a
T
-immersion, we need to give an example of a monoid morphism whose kernel is zero but that is not injective. So consider the addition on the natural numbers
\operatorname{add}_{\N}:\N\oplus\N\to\N
. Note
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{add}_{\N})=0
; however
\operatorname{add}_{\N}
is not injective since, for example,
\operatorname{add}_{\N}(2,3)=2+3=5=3+2=\operatorname{add}_{\N}(3,2).
Thus
\operatorname{add}_{\N}
is
T
-unramified but not a
T
-immersion.

7.
Tangent-Submersions

We now study the dual property of a map being an immersion, that is, being a submersion. Submersions are a very important class of maps in both differential geometry and tangent category theory because they
satisfy many remarkable and useful properties. For instance, geometrically the submersions in
\SMan
encapsulate exactly the smooth morphism
f:X\to Y
which meet
all
maps
g
in the maximal sieve
\SMan(-,Y)
transversely. Categorically, the submersions in
\SMan
are precisely the
T
-display morphisms
10
10

10

Those maps which admit pullbacks against arbitarary morphismas and for which those pullbacks are preserved by all powers of the tangent functor.
in the sense of
[
CL25
, Theorem 2.31]
. Finally, in an application of categorical reasoning to differential geometry, defining what it means to be a Lie groupoid also requires the use of surjective submersions: a Lie groupoid is a groupoid
\Gbb
internal to
\SMan
for which the source and target maps are surjective submersions.

Alternatively to working in differential geometry, we will see below in algebraic contexts that meeting arbitrary maps in maximally transverse ways is much more subtle than the concept initially indicates. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
for a commutative rig
R
we will find that
f:A\to B
is a submersion precisely when it is surjective with a linear section. Additionally, when given a commutative rig
R
and a map
f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
, in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
we will see that
f
is a submersion precisely when
\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
is a split extension of
\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}
by
\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B
in
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
. When
R
is a commutative ring, this is related to studying when the map
f
is formally smooth relative to
R
. What is common to all perspectives, however, is that submersions arise from weak pullbacks in each respective tangent category.

7.1.
Weak Pullbacks and Submersions

In the general case we study submersions following the paths laid down by
[
Mac21
]
,
[
Gal18
]
, and
[
CL25
]
: submersions are maps for which the projection naturality square is a weak pullback which is preserved by all powers of the tangent bundle functor. To this end we will proceed by first recalling what it means to be a weak pullback. In a category
\Cscr
, a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

is a
weak pullback in
\Cscr
if for any diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

there is at least one map such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\geq 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

Definition 7.1.1

(
[
Mac21
, Definition 9]
)
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, a map
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-submersion if the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a weak pullback which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
.

In order to prove some key facts about
T
-submersions, we will need the following folklorish result about weak pullbacks, which we prove for completeness sake.

Lemma 7.1.2

(Folklore)
.

Consider commutative diagrams with squares numbered as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

(1)

(2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

(3)

Then:

(i)

If Squares
(1)
and
(2)
are weak pullbacks then so too is Square
(3)
.

(ii)

If Square
(3)
is a weak pullback and if Square
(2)
is a prepullback then Square
(1)
is a weak pullback.

In particular, if Square
(2)
is a pullback then Square
(1)
is a weak pullback if and only if Square
(3)
is a weak pullback.

Proof.

Note that the final statement of the lemma follows by combining both
(i)
and
(ii)
together with the fact that pullbacks are both weak pullbacks and prepullbacks. As such, it suffices to prove
(i)
and
(ii)
to prove the lemma. We first prove
(i)
. Assume that we have a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

and note that this implies that in particular we obtain a commuting diagram with a lift
\lambda

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\circ t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

by virtue of Square
(2)
being a weak pullback. But then since
g\circ\lambda=k^{\prime}\circ t
, the fact that Square
(1)
is a weak pullback allows us to produce a map
\rho:P\to X
through which
\lambda
and
t
are factored by
k
and
f
, respectively. A straightforward check shows that
\rho
renders the entire diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

commutative. Because the outer edges exactly compose to the initial cone above Square
(3)
, this shows that Squre
(3)
is a weak pullback.

Now for
(ii)
, assume that we have a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}

and note that by post-composing this with Square
(2)
we get a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\,\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}\circ k^{\prime}}

where
\lambda
exists precisely because Square
(3)
is a weak pullback. Since
f\circ\lambda=t
, we also have that
g\circ k\circ\lambda=k^{\prime}\circ f\circ\lambda=k^{\prime}\circ t
. Similarly, by the assumption
g\circ s=k^{\prime}\circ t
, we see that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell^{\prime}}

commutes with both presented lifts
s
and
k\circ\lambda
. However, because Square
(2)
is a prepullback, we must have that
k\circ\lambda=s
and so that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}

commutes. Thus Square
(1)
is a weak pullback.
∎

Proposition 7.1.3
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
be a
T
-submersion. Then:

(i)

If
g:Y\to Z
is a
T
-sumbersion then
g\circ f:X\to Z
is a
T
-submersion.

(ii)

If
m\in\N
then
T^{m}f
is a
T
-submersion.

(iii)

Assume
g:Z\to Y
is an arbitrary map for which the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

exists and is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
. Then
\pi_{1}
is a
T
-submersion.

Proof.

Starting with
(i)
, note that for all
m\in\N
in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}g}

appealing to Lemma
7.1.2
.(i) gives the result because both the left-and-right-handed squares are weak pullbacks by assumption. For
(ii)
, observe that this is immediate (but tedious to verify) from the fact that each functor
T^{m}
preserves the weak pullbacks defining what it means to be a
T
-submersion, using that we get various natural isomorphisms
T^{m+k}\ast p\cong T^{k}\ast\left(T^{m}\ast p\right)\cong T^{k}\ast\left(p\ast T^{m}\right)
for all
k\in\N
by appropriately whiskering the powers of the tangent functors by various (whiskered) canonical flips, and using that being a weak pullback is stable under isomorphism. For
(iii)
, consider that by the final statement of Lemma
7.1.2
, in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

(1)

Square
(1)
is a weak pullback if and only if the total square is a weak pullback. However, as the total square is equal to the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

it suffices to prove that this rectangle is a weak pullback. To this end assume we have a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and note that by reorganizing our composition we get a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\,\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

with the existence of
\gamma
justified by the fact that the naturality square of
p
at
f
is a weak pullback. But then because
TW
is a pullback of
Tf
against
Tg
, we see that there is a unique morphism
\theta:U\to TW
rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}

commutative. This then implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\,\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ T\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ Tg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes and so that the whole diagram is a weak pullback. Appealing again to the final statement of Lemma
7.1.2
we get that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TW}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TZ}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

is a weak pullback. Running this same argument for all
m\in\N
and using that the powers of the tangent functor
T^{m}
preserve every structure weak pullback and pullback in sight allows us to conclude that
\pi_{1}
is a
T
-submersion.
∎

As we can see in Definition
7.1.1
above,
T
-submersions are defined in more general situations than when the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
is necessarily defined. This is not an issue in practice because conventional practice favours working with display
T
-submersions, such as in
[
Mac21
, Proposition 2]
and
[
CL25
, Proposition 2.28]
