for instance. In order to relate
T
-submersions to the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
, we need to assume that the map
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable. In this situation we find that a map is a
T
-submersion if and only if its horizontal descent admits a section.

Proposition 7.1.4
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable morphism, then
f
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
\theta_{f}
admits a section.

Proof.

\implies
: Assume that
f
is a
T
-submersion. Because the pullback projections from
f^{\ast}(TY)
form a cone over the cospan
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xleftarrow{p_{Y}}TY
, there is a map
s
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. Because
s
is a cone morphism and
f^{\ast}(TY)
is the terminal object in the category of cones,
\theta_{f}\circ s=\id
. So
\theta_{f}
admits a section as desired.

\impliedby:
Assume that
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
is a section of
\theta_{f}
, i.e.,
s
is a map making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

commute. Then to see that the naturality square for
p
is a weak pullback define
t:Z\to TX
by the formula
t:=s\circ\gamma
. To see this defines a morphism of cones, consider that since we have the commuting diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

then

p_{X}\circ t=p_{X}\circ s\circ\gamma=p_{X}\circ s\circ\gamma=\pr_{0}\circ\gamma=h

and

Tf\circ t=Tf\circ s\circ\gamma=\pr_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s\circ\gamma=\pr_{1}\circ\gamma=g,

so
t
is indeed a cone morphism. Finally we compute that

\theta_{f}\circ t=\theta_{f}\circ s\circ\gamma=\gamma,

so there is indeed a factorization of
\gamma
through
TX
. In particular, this shows that the naturality square for
p
at
f
is a weak pullback. We now verify that for arbitrary
m\in\N
, the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

is a weak pullback. However, because this is given by applying
T^{m}
to the naturality square for
f
at
p
and because
T^{m}
preserves the pullback
f^{\ast}(TY)
,
T^{m}(s)
is a section for
T^{m}(\theta_{f})
because split idempotents are absolute coequalizers. Appealing to Corollary
3.2.12
and writing
\theta_{T^{m}f}=\tilde{C}_{m}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ C_{m}
, defining
s_{T^{m}s}:=C_{m}^{-1}\circ T^{m}s\circ\tilde{C}_{m}^{-1}
gives that

\theta_{T^{m}f}\circ s_{T^{m}f}=\tilde{C}_{m}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ C_{m}\circ C_{m}^{-1}\circ T^{m}s\circ\tilde{C}_{m}^{-1}=\tilde{C}_{m}\circ T^{m}(\theta_{f}\circ s)\circ\tilde{C}_{m}^{-1}=\tilde{C}_{m}\circ\tilde{C}_{m}^{-1}=\id.

Thus each such square is a weak pullback.
∎

We now present
T
-submersions in our main examples. Note that because classifying
T
-submersions in affine schemes is quite involved and subtle, it will involve a nontrivial digression from the abstract development of
T
-submersions, and thus have seperated this out into its own subsection below.

Example 7.1.5
.

In
\SMan
, the
T
-submersions are precisely the submersions in the usual sense (when we have access to the Axiom of Choice). To see this, note that since
\theta_{f}
is linear as a morphism of differential bundles,
\theta_{f}
is stalkwise a linear map of vector spaces. Observe also that
\theta_{f}
is stalkwise epimorphic in the category of
\R
-vector spaces and so stalkwise given by surjections of vector spaces. Finally, from the fact that the Abelian category
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Vect}
is semisimple (under the Axiom of Choice) and every epimorphism of real vector spaces splits, we can stalkwise define a linear section
s_{x}:T_{f(x)}Y\to T_{x}X
of
\theta_{f,x}
for all
x\in X
. Recall from Example
3.2.2
that
\theta_{f}\left(x,\overrightarrow{v}\right)=\left(x,D[f](x)\cdot\overrightarrow{v}\right)
for all tangent vectors
(x,\overrightarrow{v})
. We can then lift the sections
s_{x}
to a map
\tilde{s}:X\times_{Y}TY\to TX
by noting that since
p_{Y}:TY\to Y
is a submersion, the pullback
f^{\ast}(TY)
is calculated by
X\times_{Y}TY=\left\{\left(x,\left(y,\overrightarrow{w}\right)\right)\in X\times TY\;|\;y=f(x)\right\}\cong\coprod_{x\in X}T_{f(x)}Y
. Since each map
s_{x}:T_{f(x)}Y\to T_{x}X
is monic and each pair
(y,\overrightarrow{w})
arising as the
TY
-coordinate in
X\times_{Y}TY
has the property that
y=f(x)
for some
x
, we get
\tilde{s}
as the unique map fitting into the coproduct diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{f(x)}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{x}}

That this is a section is immediate to verify and that it is linear follows from the fact that each stalk
s_{x}
is linear.

Example 7.1.6
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
the
T
-submersions are precisely the surjections
f:A\to B
with a linear section. To see this recall that given the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f[\epsilon]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the pullback
A\times_{B}B[\epsilon]\cong A\ltimes B
with the map
\theta_{f}:A[\epsilon]\to A\ltimes B
given by
\theta_{f}(a+x\epsilon)=(a,f(x))
. Now observe that any map
\sigma:A\ltimes B\to A[\epsilon]
takes the form
\sigma(r,s)=\sigma_{0}(r,s)+\sigma_{1}(r,s)\epsilon
for some maps
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}:A\ltimes B\to A
. Thus if there is a
\sigma
rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\ltimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[\epsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\ltimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

commutative then we have that
\id_{A\ltimes B}=\theta_{f}\circ\sigma
. This allows us to deduce:

\id_{A\ltimes B}=\theta_{f}\circ\sigma

\forall\,(a,b)\in A\ltimes B.\,(a,b)=\theta_{f}(\sigma_{0}(a,b)+\sigma_{1}(a,b)\epsilon)=(\sigma_{0}(a,b),f(\sigma_{1}(a,b)))

a=\sigma_{0}(a,b)
and
b=f(\sigma_{1}(a,b))
.

Thus it must be the case that for every
b
there is an
x\in A
(namely
x=\sigma_{1}(a,b)
) for which
f(x)=b
. This implies in particular that
f
is surjective. We now show that
\sigma_{1}
must be a derivation. Note that since
\sigma
is a rig morphism, we deduce:

\forall\,(a,b),(x,y)\in A\ltimes B.\,\sigma(a,b)+\sigma(x,y)=\sigma((a,b)+(x,y))=\sigma(a+x,b+y)

\sigma_{0}(a+x,b+y)=\sigma_{0}(a,b)+\sigma_{0}(x,y)
and
\sigma_{1}(a,b)+\sigma_{1}(x,y)=\sigma_{1}(a+x,b+y)

Thus
\sigma_{1}((a,b)+(x,y))=\sigma_{1}(a,b)+\sigma_{1}(x,y)
and in particular
\sigma_{1}(0,0)=0
, showing that is additive. Additionally because
\sigma
is a rig morphism we deduce:

\forall\,(a,b),(x,y)\in A\ltimes B.\,\sigma(a,b)\sigma(x,y)=\sigma((a,b)(x,y))

(\sigma_{0}(a,b)+\sigma_{1}(a,b)\epsilon)(\sigma_{0}(x,y)+\sigma_{1}(x,y)\epsilon))=\sigma(ax,f(a)y+bf(x))

\sigma_{0}(a,b)\sigma_{1}(x,y)+\sigma_{1}(a,b)\sigma_{0}(x,y)=\sigma_{1}(ax,f(a)y+bf(x))

Finally, because rig morphisms preserve multiplicative units, we also deduce:

\sigma(1,0)=1_{A[\epsilon]}

1+0\epsilon=\sigma(1,0)=\sigma_{0}(1,0)+\sigma_{1}(1,0)\epsilon

\sigma_{1}(1,0)=0

This in particular shows that
\sigma_{1}:A\ltimes B\to A
is a derivation. Furthermore, consider the map
s:B\to A
defined via the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\ltimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\mapsto(0,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{1}}

of
A
-modules; note that the map
b\mapsto(0,b)
is a map of
A
-modules because for all
a\in A
and for all
b\in B

a\bullet(0,b)=(a,0)(0,b)=(a\cdot 0,f(a)b+0^{2})=(0,a\bullet b).

We compute that for all
b\in B
on one hand

\displaystyle(\theta_{f}\circ s)(b)=\big(\theta_{f}\circ\sigma\big)(\operatorname{incl}(b))=\operatorname{incl}(b)=(0,b)

while on the other hand

\displaystyle(\theta_{f}\circ s)(b)=\theta_{f}\big(\sigma(\operatorname{incl}(b))\big)=\theta_{f}\big(\sigma_{1}(0,b)\epsilon\big)=\theta_{f}\big(0+\sigma_{1}(0,b)\epsilon\big)=\bigg(0,f\big(\sigma_{1}(0,b)\big)\bigg)=\bigg(0,f\big(s(b)\big)\bigg).

Thus we conclude, by comparing
B
-coordinates, that
f(s(b))=b
and so that
s:B\to A
is a linear section of
A
.

Example 7.1.7
.

In a CDC, for a map
f:X\to Y
, because
\theta_{f}=\langle\pi_{0},D[f]\rangle
, we get that any section
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
has the form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle s_{0},s_{1}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{0},D[f]\rangle}

This tells us immediately that
s_{0}=\id_{X}
. It also tells us that
D[f]\circ s_{1}=\id_{Y}
and so shows that it is necessary and sufficient for
D[f]
to be a retract. In particular, if
f
is linear and hence
\theta_{f}=\id_{X}\times f
, then
f
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
f
is a retract.

7.2.
Submersions in Algebraic Geometry

In order to classify the
T
-submersions in the categories
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
(and more generally in
\Sch_{/S}
for a base scheme
S
) we need to recall what it means for a morphism
f:A\to B
of commutative rings to be formally smooth. We also need to recall a weaker and less-well-known notion of formal smoothness which commutative rings can satisfy developed in
[
GD64
]
. This is a notion which defines when a commutative algebra morphism
g:A\to B
is formally smooth relative to a base ring
R
; it is a relatively weak definition and, despite the way the term “relative to
(-)
” is used in all of EGA and SGA,
not
the same thing as
g:A\to B
being formally smooth in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
.

Definition 7.2.1

(
[
GD64
, Définition 19.3.1]
)
.

Lef
f:A\to B
be a morphism of commutative rings. We say that
f
is
formally smooth
if
f
satisfies the weak left lifting property against nilpotent ideal quotient maps. That is, given any commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C}{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

with
I\trianglelefteq C
a nilpotent ideal, there exists a ring map
\ell:B\to C
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C}{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

commute. If instead the diagrams and lifts above occur in the category
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
for a commutative ring
R
, then
f
is
a formally smooth map of
R
-algebras
.

An important way to view formally smooth maps is in terms of their characterization by Kähler differentials via the Jacobian Criterion for Formal Smoothness. This allows one to use the splitting of the relative conormal sequence to detect formal smoothness — it says that a map is formally smooth if and only if the map is formally nonsingular in the sense that locally/for a choice of local coordinates the Jacobian determinants of the transformation is invertible.

Theorem 7.2.2

(Jacobian Criterion for Formal Smoothness;
[
GD64
, Théorème 22.6.1]
,
[
Eis95
, Proposition 16.12]
,
[
Sta24
, Proposition 10.138.8]
)
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism of commutative rings and let
h:A\to P
be a formally smooth
A
-algebra with a surjection
p:P\to B
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

commute. Then
f:A\to B
is formally smooth (in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R})
if and only if the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\operatorname{Ker}(p)}{\operatorname{Ker}(p)^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{P}/{A}}\otimes_{P}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is split exact in
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
.

As we will see below, formally smooth maps relative to a base
R
are distinct in that they are characterized not by the splitting of the conormal sequence but instead by the splitting of the cotangent sequence.

Definition 7.2.3

(
[
GD64
, Définition 19.9.1]
)
.

Let
R\xrightarrow{f}A\xrightarrow{g}B
be maps of commutative rings. We say that
g:A\to B
is a
formally smooth
A
-algebra relative to
R
if and only if for any commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C}{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{A}
, if there is a lift
\ell_{R}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C}{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{R}}

commute in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
then there is a lift
\ell_{A}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C}{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{A}}

commute.

The way to view formally smooth maps relative to a base is in terms of their characterization by way of the cotangent sequence. However, we will use this below to give a cautionary example showing that formally smooth morphisms relative to a base
R
need
not
be formally smooth morphisms (even in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
). It is true, however, that formally smooth maps are formally smooth relative to
R
: this follows from a straightforward check directly from definitions by reusing any given lift
\ell
provided by the formally smooth map.

Theorem 7.2.4

(
[
GD64
, Théorème 20.5.7.ii]
)
.

Let
R\xrightarrow{f}A\xrightarrow{g}B
be maps of commutative rings. The map
g:A\to B
is formally smooth relative to
R
if and only if the relative cotangent sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

is split exact.

Example 7.2.5
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. By virtue of the equivalence
\DBun(\Spec R)\simeq{R}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}^{\op}
asserted by
[
CL23
]
, we deduce the following chains of equivalences:

\theta_{f}:T_{B/R}\to f^{\ast}(T_{A/R})
has a section in
\DBun(\Spec B)

\theta_{f}:\Spec(\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}))\to\Spec(\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}))\times_{A}\Spec B
has a section in
\DBun(\Spec B)

\theta_{f}:\Spec(\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}))\to\Spec(\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B))
has a section in
\DBun(\Spec B)

\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\to\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
admits a retract in
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}

0\to\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\to\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{B}}\to 0
is split exact in
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}

As such, invoking
[
GD64
, Théorème 20.5.7.ii]
gives that
g:A\to B
is formally smooth relative to
R
if and only if
\Spec g:\Spec B\to\Spec A
is a
T
-submersion in
\mathbf{AffSch}_{/R}\simeq\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
.

Example 7.2.6
.

It is unfortunately not the case that
T
-submersions in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
coincide with formally smooth morphisms of rings. To see an explicit example of this, note that for any commutative ring
R
,
\Omega^{1}_{{R}/{R}}\cong 0
so
\Sym_{R}(\Omega^{1}_{{R}/{R}})\cong R
and
T_{R/R}\cong R
. In particular, for any commutative
R
-algebra
A
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{R/R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{A}}

is a pullback diagram. But then
!_{A}
is always a
T
-submersion because the natural map
\theta_{!_{A}}=p_{A}:T_{A/R}\to\Spec A
is a retract with linear section
0_{A}:\Spec A\to T_{A/R}
. As such, in particular, any non-smooth
R
-algebra
A
is a
T
-submersion over
R
. For a very explicit example, set
R=\Fbb_{2}
and
A=\Fbb_{2}[x]/(x^{2})
with the evident algebra map. Then because a morphism of rings is smooth if and only if it is formally smooth and finitely presented, it suffices to check that
\Fbb_{2}[x]/(x^{2})
is not a formally smooth
\Fbb_{2}
-algebra. However, as

\operatorname{Jac}\left(\frac{\Fbb_{2}[x]}{(x^{2})}\right)=\det\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\mathrm{d}(x^{2})}{\mathrm{d}x}\end{pmatrix}=\det(0)=0

the algebra
\Fbb_{2}[x]/(x^{2})
is not smooth because it violates the Jacobian Criterion.

Due to some technical difficulties which may be avoided by Proposition
7.3.1
, we defer computing the
T
-submersions in
\Sch_{/S}
until Example
7.3.4
below.

Example 7.2.7
.

If
R
is a commutative rig instead of a commutative ring, it is still true from
[
CL23
]
and Corollary
7.3.3
below
11
11

11

The referenced corollary here allows us to show that in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
,
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
admits a section in
\Cscr_{/X}
if and only if it admits a section in
\DBun(X)
. Note that it does not say that every section is linear, but instead that every section has a linear companion.
and the equivalence of categories
\DBun(A)^{\op}\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
(for all commutative
R
-algebras
A
) that for a map
f:B\to A
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
,
\theta_{f}:T_{B/R}\to f^{\ast}(T_{A/R})
admits a section if and only if the map of
B
-modules
\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\to\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
admits a retract
r:\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}\to\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B
in the category
{B}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
.

7.3.
Linear Submersions

Part of the reason that we have spent a great deal of time defining and studying the structure of
p
-carrable morphisms in this paper is that the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
allows us to use differntial bundles as a geometric tool with which to study how close or how far
f
is from being a categorical local diffeomorphism. We have seen already that when studying
p
-carrable morphisms which are also
T
-submersions, we have access to a section/retraction pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

However, there is one major structural question which underlies
p
-carrable
T
-submersions at every corner: how linear is the section
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
? Certainly
s
is a bundle map, as since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

also commutes. However, it need not be the case that
s
itself is directly linear. What we instead ask is the following general question: “given a
p
-carrable
T
-submersion
f:X\to Y
, if we have corresponding section
s
to
\theta_{f}
, can we build a
linear
section of
\theta_{f}
out of
s
?” We will present some partial answers to this question below and indicate that, for the case of Rosický tangent categories and for the opposite categories of commutative algebras
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
over a commutative rig
R
, it is the case that we can find a “linearization” or “linear component” of
s
, of sorts.

The first cases we consider for building linear versions of given sections are those which mirror the local algebraic structure that the symmetric algebra
\Sym_{X}
and underlying module
\operatorname{Und}_{X}
induce, via the opposite equivalences
\DBun(\Spec A)^{\op}\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
of
[
CL23
]
, on the corresponding differential bundle categories
\Sym_{X}^{\op}:\DBun(X)^{\op}\to\mathbf{CAlg}_{X}^{\op}
and
\operatorname{Und}_{X}^{\op}:\mathbf{CAlg}_{X}^{\op}\to\DBun(X)^{\op}
. The point here is that there are three key ingredients to this situation:

(i)

In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, every map is
p
-carrable.

(ii)

In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
,
\Sym_{A}
(the functor whose opposite forgets the differential bundle over
A
structure) is left adjoint to the underlying module functor
\operatorname{Und}_{A}
(the functor whose opposite takes a sliced object and promotes it to a differential bundle).

(iii)

The unit of adjunction
M\to\operatorname{Und}_{A}(\Sym_{A}(M))
is a pointwise split monic in
{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}
, i.e., the left adjoint
\Sym_{A}
is faithful.

Abstracting these appropriately and taking opposites where indicated allows us to produce the following situation in which we know we can produce linear sections out of general sections.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
be a map such that:

(i)

The forgetful functor
\operatorname{Forget}_{X}:\DBun(X)\to\Cscr_{/X}
has a left adjoint
F_{X}:\Cscr_{/X}\to\DBun(X)
.

(ii)

The counit of adjunction
\epsilon
for
F_{X}\dashv\operatorname{Forget}_{X}
is a pointwise split epimorphism.

(iii)

The map
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable.

Then for any section of
\theta_{f}
,
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
, there admits a linear section
s_{f}
of
\theta_{f}
(so a section in
\DBun(X)
). In particular, if
f
is a
T
-submersion then there exists a linear section of
\theta_{f}
witnessing that
f
is a
T
-submersion.

Proof.

Begin by observing that since
\theta_{f}
is a linear morphism of differential bundles over
X
, we can rewrite the diagram expressing that
s
is a section
s
as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(TX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(\theta_{f})}

Now apply the left adjoint
F_{X}
to the diagram above to produce the commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}\left(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY))\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}\left(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(TX)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}\left(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(\theta_{f})}

Let
\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to F_{X}(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY)))
denote a given splitting of the counit

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}\left(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}\big(f^{\ast}(TY)\big)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{f^{\ast}(TY)}}

and define the linear map of differential bundles
s_{f}
via the composition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(f^{\ast}(TY)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(TX))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X}(s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{TX}}

We then compute that

\displaystyle\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}

\displaystyle=\theta_{f}\circ\epsilon_{TX}\circ F_{X}(s)\circ\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}=\epsilon_{f^{\ast}(TY)}\circ F_{X}\left(\operatorname{Forget}_{X}(\theta_{f})\right)\circ F_{X}(s)\circ\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}=\epsilon_{f^{\ast}(TY)}\circ\sigma_{f^{\ast}(TY)}

\displaystyle=\id_{f^{\ast}(TY)}

so
s_{f}
is indeed a section to
\theta_{f}
. Moreover,
s_{f}
is linear exactly because it is defined as a composite of maps in
\DBun(X)
. The final claim of the proposition is immediate once we know that all powers
T^{m}
preserve the weak pullback which makes
f
a
T
-submersion.
∎

Corollary 7.3.2
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable map such that:

(i)

The forgetful functor
\operatorname{Forget}_{X}:\DBun(X)\to\Cscr_{/X}
has a left adjoint
F_{X}:\Cscr_{/X}\to\DBun(X)
.

(ii)

The counit of adjunction
\epsilon
for
F_{X}\dashv\operatorname{Forget}_{X}
is a pointwise split epimorphism.

Then the horizontal descent map
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
admits a section if and only if it admits a
linear
section.

We now show that both our tangent categories of affine schemes and schemes satisfy the conditions of the corollary.

Corollary 7.3.3
.

The categories
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
(for a commutative rig
R
) and
\Sch_{/S}
(for a base scheme
S
) have the property that for any map
f:X\to Y
, the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
admits a section if and only if it admits a linear section.

Proof.

In both cases the functor
\operatorname{Forget}_{X}:\DBun(X)\to\Cscr_{/X}
arises either as

\operatorname{Forget}_{X}\cong\underline{\Spec}_{X}(\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}(-)):\QCoh(X)^{\op}\to\Sch_{/X}

in the case of an
S
-scheme
X
or as

\operatorname{Forget}_{X}\cong\Sym_{X}^{\op}(-):{X}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}^{\op}\to\mathbf{CAlg}_{X}^{\op}=(\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op})_{/X}.

when
X
is an object of
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
. In both cases the functors

\Sym_{X}:{X}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}\to\mathbf{CAlg}_{X},\qquad\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}}:\QCoh(X)\to\QCoh(X,\mathbf{CAlg}_{\Ocal_{X}})

are left adjoints whose unit
\eta
is pointwise split. Taking the opposite of the rig-theoretic case or the relative spectrum of the scheme-theoretic case implies that
\operatorname{Forget}_{X}
is a right adjoint with a pointwise split counit in all cases.
∎

Example 7.3.4
.

Let
S
be a base scheme. Then in
\Sch_{/S}
a map
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-submersion if and only if the relative cotangent sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{f}}

is split exact in
\QCoh(X)
. This follows from the fact that
(\underline{\Spec}_{X}\circ\underline{\Sym}_{\Ocal_{X}})(v_{f})=\theta_{f}
and by Corollary
7.3.3
,
\theta_{f}
admits a section if and only if
v_{f}
admits a section in
\QCoh(X)^{\op}
.

We now show that in a Rosický tangent category, we can build linear sections from any section of the horizontal descent of a
p
-carrable map. We will need to do this in two steps: first we will show that in a Rosický tangent category if
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
is a section of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
, the map
s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
is a section of
\theta_{f}
which preserves the zero sections of the bundle map. Second we will have to show that if we have a zero-preserving section
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
then there is a corresponding linear section
s_{f}
of
\theta_{f}
.

Lemma 7.3.5
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable map in a Rosický tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Then if
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
is a section of the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
, the map
s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}
is a section of
\theta_{f}
which preserves the zero section of
f^{\ast}(TY)
and
TX
.

Proof.

Begin by observing that by assumption the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

commutes and so
s
is a map in the slice category
\Cscr_{/X}
. We also compute that in
\Cscr_{/X}

p_{X}\circ(s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0})=p_{X}\circ s-p_{X}\circ s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}\circ f^{\ast}\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}-\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}

where the last equality holds because
\pi_{0}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to X
is the map from
f^{\ast}(TY)
to the terminal object in
\Cscr_{/X}
. We also compute that

(s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0})\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=0_{X}

which shows that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(0_{Y})}

commutes. Note that the last equality uses the fact that
0_{X}
is the unit of the bundle structure on
TX
and the fact that the difference of the maps
\alpha-\alpha
of morphisms
\alpha:X\to TX
has the property that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha-\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

commutes. Finally we also compute that

\theta_{f}\circ(s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0})=\theta_{f}\circ s-\theta_{f}\circ s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=\id-\theta_{f}\circ 0_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=\id-f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=\id

because
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to f^{\ast}(TY)
is the zero map on
f^{\ast}(TY)
. Thus
s-s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}
is a section to the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}
which preserves the zero section
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})
of
f^{\ast}(TY)
.
∎

Proposition 7.3.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable map in a Rosický tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
. Suppose that the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
admits a section
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
which preserves the zero section, i.e.,
s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=0_{X}
. Then there exists a linear section
s_{f}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
of
\theta_{f}
.

Proof.

We construct the section
s_{f}
as follows. Recall that from the universal property of the vertical lift, we get that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

is an equalizer diagram. We will use this universal property to show that we can induced a unique section
s_{f}
from
f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
which equalizes the three listed maps. To this end we must first prove that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}

commutes where
\lambda=f^{\ast}(\ell_{Y})
is the lift of the differential bundle structure on
f^{\ast}(TY)
over
X
. We first compute that by virtue of
\lambda=f^{\ast}(\ell_{Y})
,

(T\ast p)_{X}\circ Ts\circ\lambda=T(p_{X}\circ s)\circ\lambda=T\pi_{0}\circ\lambda=0_{X}\circ\pi_{0}

and

0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ(T\ast p)_{X}\circ Ts\circ\lambda=0_{X}\circ p_{X}\circ 0_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=(0\ast T)_{X}\circ\pi_{0}

Finally, because
s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=0_{X}
, we also get that

(p\ast T)_{X}\circ Ts\circ\lambda=s\circ p_{X}\circ\lambda=s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=0_{X}\circ\pi_{0}.

Applying the universal property of the equalizer gives rise to a unique map
s_{f}
rendering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}(TY))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts}

We will prove that
s_{f}
is the section we desire.

Let us first prove that
s_{f}
is a linear bundle morphism. First by construction we have the following commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}(TY))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ts}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{f^{\ast}(TY)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T)_{X}}

We additionally have a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(0_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}

because
s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=0_{X}
. We also compute on one hand that
p_{X}=p_{X}\circ(p\ast T)_{X}\circ\ell_{X}
and that
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=p_{f^{\ast}(TY)}\circ\lambda
. Putting these together gives

\displaystyle p_{X}\circ s_{f}

\displaystyle=p_{X}\circ(p\ast T)_{X}\circ\ell_{X}\circ s_{f}=p_{X}\circ(p\ast T)_{X}\circ Ts\circ\lambda=p_{X}\circ s\circ p_{f^{\ast}(TY)}\circ\lambda

\displaystyle=p_{X}\circ s\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})\circ\pi_{0}=p_{X}\circ 0_{X}\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}.

Thus the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}

commutes and so
s_{f}
is a bundle map. To see that it is a linear bundle map we note that
\ell_{X}
is
T
-monic by Proposition
5.0.8
and so, in particular,
T\ell_{X}=(T\ast\ell)_{X}
is monic. We use this to deduce that

\displaystyle T\ell_{X}\circ Ts_{f}\circ\lambda

\displaystyle=T^{2}s\circ T\lambda\circ\lambda=T^{2}s\circ\ell_{f^{\ast}(TY)}\circ\lambda=(\ell\ast T)_{X}\circ Ts\circ\lambda=(\ell\ast T)_{X}\circ\ell_{X}\circ s_{f}

\displaystyle=T\ell_{X}\circ\ell_{X}\circ s_{f}.

Using that
T\ell_{X}
is monic gives that
\ell_{X}\circ s_{f}=Ts_{f}\circ\lambda
and hence that
s_{f}
is a linear bundle map.

We now prove that
s_{f}
is a section of
\theta_{f}
. To see this note that since
f^{\ast}(TY)
is a pullback, it suffices to prove that
\pi_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=\pi_{0}
and that
\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=\pi_{1}
. For the first equation we derive that

\pi_{0}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=p_{X}\circ s_{f}=\pi_{0}

because
s_{f}
is a linear bundle map. For the second note that
\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=Tf\circ s_{f}
and that
\ell_{Y}\circ Tf\circ s_{f}=s_{f}\circ\ell_{X}\circ T^{2}f
. Since
\lambda:f^{\ast}(TY)\to T(f^{\ast}(TY))
has the form
\lambda=\langle 0_{X}\circ\pi_{0},\ell_{Y}\circ\pi\rangle
by
[
CC18
, Bottom of Page 12]
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(TY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(f^{\ast}(TY))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi_{1}}

commutes. This allows us to deduce that on one hand
\ell_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}=T\pi_{1}\circ\lambda
while on the other hand, since
Tf\circ s=\pi_{1}
by virtue of
s
being a section to
\theta_{f}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}

\displaystyle=\ell_{Y}\circ Tf\circ s_{f}=T^{2}f\circ\ell_{X}\circ s_{f}=T^{2}f\circ Ts\circ\lambda=T(Tf\circ s)\circ\lambda=T\pi_{1}\circ\lambda.

Thus
\ell_{Y}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=T\pi_{1}\circ\lambda=\ell_{1}\circ\pi_{1}
and so, once again by
\ell_{Y}
being
T
-monic by Proposition
5.0.8
, we have that
\pi_{1}\circ\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=\pi_{1}
. This in turn allows us to deduce that
\theta_{f}\circ s_{f}=\id_{f^{\ast}TY}
and so proves that
s_{f}
is a linear section of
\theta_{f}
.
∎

Putting these results together we can deduce the following: in a Rosický tangent category every
p
-carrable
T
-submersion admits a
linear
section of the horizontal descent, not just a nonlinear section.

Corollary 7.3.7
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
be a Rosický tangent category and let
f:X\to Y
be a
p
-carrable morphism. Then the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:TX\to f^{\ast}(TY)
admits a section in
\Cscr_{/X}
if and only if it admits a section in
\DBun(X)
. In particular,
f
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
\theta_{f}
is a retract in
\DBun(X)
.

Proof.

For the
\implies
direction, assume that
\theta_{f}
admits a section
s:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
in
\Cscr_{/X}
. Then applying Lemma
7.3.5
shows that there is a section
\hat{s}:f^{\ast}(TY)\to TX
in
\Cscr_{/X}
which preserves the zero section
f^{\ast}(0_{Y})
in the sense that
\hat{s}\circ f^{\ast}(0_{Y})=0_{X}
. Finally applying Proposition
7.3.6
gives that there is a linear section
s_{f}
of
\theta_{f}
, i.e., a section
s_{f}
to
\theta_{f}
in
\DBun(X)
. For the
\impliedby
direction, this is immediate because linear sections are, in particular, sections upon forgetting from
\DBun(X)
to
\Cscr_{/X}
. The final claim of the corollary follows from an immediate application of Proposition
7.1.4
.
∎

Corollary 7.3.8
.

Each of the tangent categories
\SMan,\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
,
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, and
\Sch_{/S}
for
R
a commutative ring have the property that
f:X\to Y
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
\theta_{f}
admits a linear section.

Proof.

Each of the above tangent categories are Rosický, so simply apply Corollary
7.3.7
in each case.
∎

8.
Tangent Étale Morphisms

We now discuss the final topic of the paper: that of
T
-étale morphisms. These are the morphisms in a tangent category which play the role of local diffeomorphisms. In fact, in
[
CL25
, Theorem 3.45]
the authors show that the monic
T
-étale maps which are also display morphisms form a class of maps which are analogous to open embeddings of smooth submanifolds in differential geometry. Because of the importance of open immersions and also because étale morphisms give the local flavour of a category of geometric objects, we are thus obliged to study
T
-étale morphisms in the same systematic way as we studied
T
-immersions and
T
-unramified morphisms.

8.1.
Étale Moprphisms and Examples

Our focus in this subsection is to get to know
T
-étale maps based on their structure, how they incarnate in our four main families of examples, and what permanence/stability properties they have. We are in particular interested in how they relate to, and may be used with,
T
-immersions and
T
-submersions. We expect that future applications of these ideas will require an intimate knowledge of what being étale in this tangent-categorical context buys.

Definition 8.1.1

(
[
CL24
, Definition 35]
,
[
CL25
, Definition 2.27]
)
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, a map
f:X\to Y
is
T
-étale
when the naturality square for
p
at
f

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a pullback diagram which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
.

The following proposition is immediate from definition. They allow us to characterize
T
-étale maps as maps which are both
T
-immersions and
T
-submersions in any tangent category and, when a map is
p
-carrable, characterize being
T
-étale in terms of the horizontal descent being an isomorphism.

Proposition 8.1.2
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
,

(i)

A map
fY
is
T
-étale if and only if
f
is both a
T
-immersion and a
T
-submersion;

(ii)

A
p
-carrable map
f
is
T
-étale if and only if
\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism. So if
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable map and
T
-étale then
f^{\ast}(TY)\cong TX
.

Proof.

For statement
(i)
, this is immediate from the fact that pullbacks are pre-pullbacks which are weak pullbacks and vice-versa. For statement
(ii)
, this immediate by uniqueness of pullbacks up to isomorphism.
∎

We now present various examples of the
T
-étale maps in our main examples.

Example 8.1.3
.

In
\SMan
, the
T
-étale maps are precisely the local diffeomorphisms. This is because a map
f:X\to Y
is a local diffeomorphism is exactly when
f
is a
T
-immersion and a
T
-submersion, and hence
T
-étale if and only if it is a local diffeomorphism. This gives a clean structural proof of the fact that in
\SMan
local diffeomorphisms are exactly the smooth morphisms whose tangent bundle projection naturality square is a pullback.

Example 8.1.4
.

In a CDC, a map
f:X\to Y
is
T
-étale if and only if
D[f]
is an isomorphism. This is because by Example
6.1.9

f
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
D[f]
is monic while by Example
7.1.7

f
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
D[f]
is a retract.

Example 8.1.5
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. Then in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
, the
T
-étale maps are precisely the isomorphisms of
R
-algebras. This is seen as follows: for any morphism
f:A\to B
of commutative
R
-algebras, recall from Example
4.1.2
that the horizontal descent
\theta_{f}:A[\epsilon]\to A\ltimes B
is given by
a+x\epsilon\mapsto(a,f(x))
. Now
\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism if and only if
f
is an isomorphism in the first place.

Example 8.1.6
.

Let
R
be a commutative rig. In
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
, a map
f^{\op}:B\to A
(so a map
f:A\to B
in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}
) is
T
-étale if and only if the map
\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\to\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
is an isomorphism of
B
-modules (which follows from Examples
6.1.7
and
7.2.5
). This follows from the equivalence of categories
\DBun(A)\simeq{A}\textnormal{-}\mathbf{Mod}^{\op}
of
[
CL23
]
together with the isomorphism of rigs
\Sym_{B}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B)\cong\Sym_{A}(\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}})\otimes_{A}B
. Note that in particular, when
R
is a commutative ring this says that the
T
-étale maps in
\mathbf{AffSch}_{/R}
are exactly the formally unramified maps of affine schemes which are also formally smooth relative to
R
. In particular, every formally unramified map
!_{A}:\Spec A\to\Spec R
over the terminal object is
T
-étale in
\mathbf{AffSch}_{/R}
by Example
7.2.6
. Note that it certainly need not be the case that
\Spec A
is formally smooth over
\Spec R
.

In the case of algebraic geometry, there is an unfortunate overlap in terminology between formally étale maps of affine schemes and
T
-étale maps of affine schemes. In particular,
these are not the same concept — being formally étale is stronger.
We now show that it is the case that formally étale maps are
T
-étale but also provide an example of a morphism in
\mathbf{CAlg}_{R}^{\op}
which is
T
-étale but
not
formally étale.

Example 8.1.7
.

Let
S
be a base scheme. In
\Sch_{/S}
, a map
f:X\to Y
is
T
-étale if and only if in the cotangent exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

the map
u
is an isomorphism. Now recall that a map
f:X\to Y
of
S
-schemes is formally étale if and only if it is formally smooth and formally unramified. By
[
GD67
, Proposition 17.2.3.ii]
, since
f
is formally smooth the cotangent sequence is a short exact sequence in
\QCoh(X)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

which is locally split. Furthermore, since
f
is formally unramified,
\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{Y}}\cong 0
and so the cotangent sequence is isomorphic to the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

which shows that
f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})\cong\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}
. Since
f:X\to Y
is
T
-étale if and only if
f^{\ast}(\Omega^{1}_{{Y}/{S}})\cong\Omega^{1}_{{X}/{S}}
, it follows that formally étale maps are
T
-étale.

Example 8.1.8
.

Here is now an example of
T
-étale morphism of schemes which is not formally étale. Let
A=R=\mathbb{C}\llbracket t\rrbracket
and let
B=\mathbb{C}\llbracket t\rrbracket/(t^{2})
with
f:A\to R
the identity morphism and
g:A\to B
the quotient map. Then
g
is not formally étale (it is not formally smooth) but
\Omega^{1}_{{A}/{R}}\otimes_{A}B\cong 0\cong\Omega^{1}_{{B}/{R}}
. Thus
g:A\to B
is
T
-étale but not formally étale.

8.2.
The Structure of Étale Morphisms

We conclude this paper with the general theory of
T
-étale morphisms in a tangent category. Our next task is to prove some stability and permanence properties of
T
-étale maps and some of their variations. Of particular interest is Proposition
8.2.2
below, as it shows that when we know a map
g:Y\to Z
is
T
-étale then the any composite
g\circ f
is
T
-étale (respectively a
T
-submersion or
T
-immersion) if and only if the same is true for
f
. After establishing this, we will focus on monic
T
-étale morphisms before concluding with a study of when tangent functors reflect étale morphisms.

Proposition 8.2.1
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be
T
-étale morphisms and assume that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

which is preserved by the tangent functor
T
. Then:

(i)

The composite
g\circ f
is
T
-étale.

(ii)

The morphism
\pr_{1}
is
T
-étale.

Proof.

For
(i)
this is the Pullback Lemma in action. For
(ii)
we observe that this is exactly
[
CL24
, Lemma 36]
.
∎

Proposition 8.2.2
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be morphisms in a tangent category for which
g
is
T
-étale. Then:

(i)

f
is a
T
-immersion if and only if
g\circ f
is a
T
-immersion.

(ii)

f
is a
T
-submersion if and only if
g\circ f
is a
T
-submersion.

(iii)

f
is
T
-étale if and only if
g\circ f
is
T
-étale.

Proof.

Fix an
m\in\N
and consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p\ast T^{m})_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}g}

Note that the right-handed square in the rectangle is a pullback by virtue of
g
being
T
-étale. We must show:

(i)

for
(i)
that the left-handed square is a prepullback if and only if the whole rectangle is;

(ii)

for
(ii)
that the left-handed square is a weak pullback if and only if the whole rectangle is;

(iii)

for
(iii)
that the left-handed square is a pullback if and only if the whole rectangle is.

However, in all cases we can reduce proving each item to applying the functor
T^{m}
to the diagram and then: for
(i)
appealing to Lemma
6.1.2
for each
m\in\N
; for
(ii)
by appealing to Lemma
7.1.2
for each
m\in\N
; and for
(iii)
, this is simply the Pullback Lemma applied at each
m\in\N
.
∎

We now give a short classification of the monic
T
-étale maps. In particular, we show that monic
T
-étale maps are exactly the
T
-monic
T
-submersions in any tangent category.

Proposition 8.2.3
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, a map
f:X\to Y
is monic
T
-étale if and only if
f
is both
T
-monic and a
T
-submersion.

Proof.

\implies:
If
f
is a monic
T
-étale morphism then for all
m\in\N
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

is a pullback. Since pullbacks are in particular weak pullbacks,
f
is a
T
-submersion. Finally, a routine induction on
m
gives that
T^{m}f
is monic for all
m\in\N
(the trick is that
T^{0}f=f
is monic by assumption and for each
k\in\N
,
T^{k+1}f
is the pullback of
T^{k}f
against
(T^{k}\ast p)_{Y}
). Thus
f
is also
T
-monic.

\impliedby:
If
f:X\to Y
is
T
-monic and a
T
-submersion then for all
m\in\N
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

is a weak pullback with each map
T^{k}f
monic for all
k\in\N
. But then since
T^{m+1}f
is monic, any diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

has
\varphi=\psi
by virtue of
T^{m+1}f
monic. Thus

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m+1}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T^{m}\ast p)_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}f}

is a weak pullback which is a prepullback and hence a pullback. Applying
T^{m}
to the naturality diagram at
p
thus is a pullback for all
m\in\N
. But arguing this for
m=0
gives that the naturality square for
p
at
f
is a pullback which is preserved by
T^{m}
for all
m\in\N
and hence that
f
is a monic
T
-étale morphism.
∎

Corollary 8.2.4
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, let
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
be monic
T
-étale morphisms and assume that we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

which is preserved by the tangent functor
T
. Then:

(i)

The composite
g\circ f
is monic
T
-étale.

(ii)

The morphism
\pr_{1}
is monic
T
-étale.

Proof.

Because monic
T
-étale maps are exactly the
T
-monic
T
-submersion maps by Proposition
8.2.3
, the result follows from the corresponding base change and composition stability results for
T
-monic and
T
-étale morphisms.
∎

Corollary 8.2.5
.

In a tangent category
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
, if a map
f:X\to Y
is
p
-carrable then
f
is monic
T
-étale if and only if
f
is monic,
\theta_{f}
is
T
-monic, and
\theta_{f}
is a retract.

Proof.

This is an application of Proposition
8.2.3
above combined with Proposition
7.1.4
and Proposition
5.0.11
.
∎

Our final task is to prove a proposition which has as a consequence that when all maps in a tangent category are
p
-carrable, then all powers of the tangent functor preserve and reflect
T
-étale maps.

Proposition 8.2.6
.

Let
(\Cscr,\Tbb)
be a tangent category such that all morphisms are
p
-carrable. Then for all
m\in\N
,
T^{m}
preserves and reflects
T
-étale morphisms, that is,
T^{m}f
is
T
-étale if and only if
f
is
T
-étale.

Proof.

So fix an
m\in\N
and let
f
be an arbitrary étale map. Applying Corollary
3.2.12
gives rise to isomorphisms
C
and
\tilde{C}
for which the identity
\theta_{T^{m}f}=\tilde{C}\circ T^{m}\theta_{f}\circ C
holds. Because
\tilde{C}
and
C
are isomorphisms,
T^{m}\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\theta_{T^{m}f}
is an isomorphism. For the
\implies
direction: if
T^{m}\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism, then applying Corollary
2.3.5
gives that
\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism as well. For the
\impliedby
direction: if
\theta_{f}
is an isomorphism then so too is
T^{m}(\theta_{f})
by virtue of
T^{m}
being a functor. But then
\theta_{T^{m}(f)}
is an isomorphism as well and so
T^{m}f
is
T
-étale.
∎
